# MTB Patent Atlas

**45 years of mountain bike invention — who patented it, who fought over it, and when it became free for everyone to use.**

An interactive timeline of mountain bike intellectual property: 372 patents and applications spanning 1869 to 2026, organized into nine categories, with dedicated views for the industry's major litigation, a live stats dashboard, four distinct visual themes, and zero build tooling required. A single HTML file that opens in any browser.

🔗 **Live:** [postmillennium-mtb.github.io/MTB-PATENT-ATLAS](https://postmillennium-mtb.github.io/MTB-PATENT-ATLAS/)
📁 **Repo:** [github.com/postmillennium-MTB/MTB-PATENT-ATLAS](https://github.com/postmillennium-MTB/MTB-PATENT-ATLAS)
✍️ A RedFoxRun / PostMillennium MTB project.

## What's in it

### Nine categories
Rear Suspension · Forks & Damping · Drivetrain · Wheels & Tires · Components · E-MTB & Electronics · Frame & Standards · Bike Transport · Cameras & Wearables

### 🔍 Search
Free-text search checks every field a card actually shows — title, assignee, summary, editorial text, patent numbers, brand/inventor tags, HQ location, category, status, badges, jurisdiction — in both languages at once, so switching languages mid-session never breaks a query already typed.

A multi-word query matches each word independently, wherever it appears on the card, not as one glued-together phrase — searching "SRAM XD" finds a card whose title says "XD" and whose assignee field says "SRAM," even though those two words never sit next to each other in that order. A compound name still matches spaced or joined ("Bike Yoke" finds "BikeYoke", "One Up" finds "OneUp").

Search also understands a small set of **synonym groups** for common mountain bike components and concepts — searching "spoke" also finds a card that only says "wheel," "hub," "freehub," or "rim," and vice versa, since the relationship works in either direction. Current groups: wheel/hub/spoke/freehub/rim, fork/stanchion/damper/telescopic, suspension/shock/linkage/damper/bath/plush, drivetrain/derailleur/chainring/cassette/shifting/chain, dropper/seatpost/post, brake/caliper/rotor, tire/tyre/tubeless/insert, pedal/cleat, frame/chassis. This only widens what a *search* matches — it never adds a word to a card's own displayed text, and a category filter chip still means exactly what its label says. See `SEARCH_SYNONYMS` in `index.html` (and CLAUDE.md's note on it) to add a group.

### 📈 Patents-per-era bar chart
A scrubber above the filter bar renders the dataset as a tappable bar chart, bracketed by year. Two toggles sit side by side in the top-right of the scrubber:

- **MTB / ALL** — **MTB** (default) scopes the chart to 1980–present, mountain biking's own era; **ALL** widens it back to the full dataset, including the 1869–1913 bicycle-mechanics entries that predate the sport. This only changes what the chart displays — the timeline's own card list underneath is never filtered by it, so switching back to MTB doesn't hide any entry, just the chart's own axis.
- **2yr / 1yr / 5yr** — the bracket size:
  - **2yr** (default) — the balance point between too dense to read and too coarse to be useful
  - **1yr** — every year gets its own bar
  - **5yr** — half-decade buckets (e.g. 1995–1999, 2000–2004 are each a single bar), for a zoomed-out view of eras

Tapping any bar jumps straight to that era's section in the timeline. Bars carry a tooltip with the exact count; a subset get a visible year label underneath, thinned automatically on narrow screens so labels never collide. Where the dataset has a real multi-decade silence (e.g. between the earliest 1890 entry and the next one in the mid-1970s), a dashed gap marker breaks the axis rather than drawing the two eras as if they were adjacent.

### ⚔ Patent Fights view
A dedicated tab frames the industry's eight major disputes as named rivalries — not just a filtered list, but head-to-head case cards showing the combatants, what the fight was actually about, how it ended, and tappable chips that jump directly to the affected patent cards in the timeline:

1. **Weagle v. Trek** — the concentric-pivot war (Split Pivot vs. ABP)
2. **Fox v. SRAM** — bleed-valve and damper fight, transferred to Colorado District Court
3. **SRAM v. the Industry** — narrow-wide chainring claims against Race Face, Fox, Praxis, Wolf Tooth
4. **Stan's v. Specialized** — Bead Socket Technology tubeless rim patent
5. **Speedplay v. Bebop** — founder-license pedal case, Federal Circuit (2000)
6. **Spot v. Gates** — belt-drive Drop-Out dropout royalty suit, currently active in Denver District Court
7. **Knolly v. Intense** — seat-tube geometry patent, resolved May 2021 on prior art
8. **RampWerks v. absoluteBLACK** — ramped/oval chainring patents, voluntarily dismissed without prejudice June 2026

### 🧭 Atlas tab (the landing view)
The first tab, and what a plain visit opens on. Built to get a reader searching, from top to bottom:
- **Pick a rabbit hole:** story cards, each showing a patent drawing, the year, the title and a one-line teaser. Tapping one opens that entry. Cards come from `RABBIT_HOLES` (hand-written, optionally pinned) first, and any empty slots are filled with random verified entries that have drawings, so the row changes on each visit. Three show on screen at a time (one on phones, peeking the next), but the row holds nine — prev/next arrows next to the heading step through the rest one card at a time, and it auto-advances every 5 seconds on its own (pausing while you're hovering or focused on it, and skipped entirely if your system has "reduce motion" on).
- **"Who invented your bike?":** a large search box whose placeholder rotates through example searches, plus "Popular" chips (`HOME_SEARCH_SUGGESTIONS`) that each show a live match count.
- The category-over-time chart (tap any colour to jump), **Start from a brand** chips, and then the full statistics section described below.
- **Four invitation cards**, below Most-patented names: still in force, expired, went to court and expiring soon. Each opens its list (or the Patent Fights tab).

Shared links keep working. `#tab=stats` opens this tab, and older filter links without a `tab=` parameter still open on the Category tab.

### 📊 Stats view
A dedicated tab that turns the raw dataset into a live dashboard: total/active/expired/litigated counts, a status-split bar, entries-by-category, a **patents-by-category-over-time chart** (below), and a most-patented-names leaderboard (brands and inventors combined, top 12). Every bar is tappable — clicking a category or a name jumps straight into the filtered timeline. Nothing here is hand-maintained; it's all computed from `D` at render time, so it can't drift out of sync the way a written-out stats table can. **The leaderboard counts atlas entries, not real-world patent holdings** — its own intro text says so, because for a handful of names (GoPro, SRAM, Shimano, Fox) the gap is large: this atlas deliberately catalogs a curated, MTB-relevant subset rather than a company's entire portfolio (see *Bundled entries*, below), so a low bar next to a famous name means "lightly represented here," not "holds few patents."

### 📈 Patents by category, over time
A stacked bar chart in the Stats tab, bucketed into fixed 5-year brackets from 1980 onward, independent of the top-of-page scrubber's 1/2/5-year toggle (the two charts serve different questions — the top chart is "how much activity in this era," this one is "what kind"). A **Count / Share** toggle switches between two readings of the same data: Count (the default) scales each bar's height to that bracket's total filing volume, so a busy era's bar is visibly taller; Share holds every bar at full height and shows only the category mix, so you can compare composition across eras — e.g. rear-suspension's early dominance giving way to newer categories — without volume differences drowning out the comparison. Either way, each colored segment is one of the eight core categories plus a ninth, "Cameras & Wearables," sized to its share; hovering a segment shows its exact count and era in a tooltip, and a legend below the chart maps every color to its category name so identity never depends on guessing a hue. The eight core category colors are a fixed, validated categorical palette (checked for colorblind-safe adjacent-pair separation, not picked by eye); "Cameras & Wearables" deliberately does *not* get a competing 9th hue — the palette method this atlas follows caps at 8 fixed hues by design, so the newest, thinnest category renders as a neutral gray "Other"-style segment instead of stretching the palette. Segments are separated by a thin gap in the surface color rather than a border, so touching categories stay visually distinct without adding ink that isn't data. Entries filed before 1980 (16 as of this writing — scattered 19th/early-20th-century bicycle-mechanics patents with decades of silence between them) aren't dropped from the chart; they're folded into one leading "**\<1980**" bar, so the regular grid stays dense and readable without a real entry disappearing from the total.

### ⏳ Going free soon
A dedicated Status filter surfaces active patents estimated to expire within the next two years — the atlas's forward-looking answer to its own tagline about technology "becoming free for everyone to use." Qualifying cards carry an amber "⏳ free by [year]" pill. The same list appears at the bottom of the Stats tab (below Most-patented names) as a tap-to-jump watchlist.

### 🔗 URL reflects the current view — and two ways to share it
Every filter combination, search term, active tab, and individual card has its own URL — the browser's address bar updates automatically as you filter and browse, and reloading or bookmarking a page returns to the same view. Individual cards resolve by patent number (or a title slug for unverified entries), so a URL can point at one specific patent. Two things put that mechanism one click away: a **🔗 Share** button in the filter toolbar (next to Surprise Me) opens a small dropdown — Copy link, or post straight to X, Facebook, or Reddit, each pre-filled with the *current* URL including whatever filters/search/open card are active — and every card also carries its own small send-icon **copy-link button** beside the expand/collapse toggle, which copies a direct link to that one entry regardless of what's currently filtered. "Copy link" and the per-card buttons share one clipboard helper that falls back through `navigator.clipboard` → a hidden-textarea `execCommand("copy")` → a manual-copy `prompt()`, since the Clipboard API is frequently blocked inside the cross-origin iframe this tool is designed to run in. The three platform options are plain share-intent link-outs (`https://x.com/intent/...` and equivalents) opened in a small popup — no SDK, no Web Share API, no added dependency. The toolbar menu is a native `<details>` (same zero-JS-to-open pattern as the "All N patents on Google Patents" disclosure on multi-patent cards) and closes itself after a click, on Escape, or on an outside click.

### 🎲 Surprise me
Jumps to a random card from whatever's currently visible (respects active filters), opens it, and scrolls it into view. A low-effort way to surface entries from deep in a 243-item catalogue that most visitors would otherwise never scroll to. The button also gives itself a subtle shimmer at a random interval (roughly every 20–90 seconds) as a passive hint that it's interactive — it never interrupts a click and turns itself off for anyone with reduced-motion preferences set.

### 📍 Colorado MTB Innovation panel
A slide-in explainer auto-linked from cards involving Colorado-connected brands (Spot, Gates, MRP, Revel, Reeb). Covers the CBF/Revel licensing story, the Spot v. Gates suit, and the Fox v. SRAM jurisdictional transfer to Colorado. Minimizes to a persistent tab; reopens with one tap. Esc key or overlay-click also minimizes.

### Four distinct visual themes
Each theme has its own structural personality — not just a different color, but different corner radii, node shapes, card borders, typography weight, and header treatment. The theme switcher lives at the top of the header, directly under the PMR link, so it's the first thing available on both desktop and mobile before scrolling into the rest of the tool:

| Theme | Club | Personality | Display font |
|---|---|---|---|
| **WMBC** | Wisconsin Mountain Bike Coalition | Clean trail / topographic — soft 14px rounded corners, round nodes, green gradient header | Nunito |
| **COMBA** | Colorado Off-Road Mountain Bicyclists | Vintage editorial print — warm cream, sharp 3px corners, square nodes, flat earthy header | Bitter |
| **CAMBA** | California Mountain Bike Association | Bold racing / high-contrast — 0px sharp corners, hard offset shadows, square pills, red left-edge accent on litigated cards | Anton |
| **CAMBR** | Chicago Area Mountain Bike Riders | Technical blueprint — navy structure, orange accents, 6px corners, mono-forward | IBM Plex Mono |

Each theme's display font drives its `--font-display` CSS variable, which
only touches headings, tab labels, and stat/fight titles — patent titles,
body copy, and buttons stay on the shared Barlow/Barlow Condensed pair for
consistent readability across themes.

### 🌐 Language switcher (English / French)
An EN/FR toggle sits in the header next to the theme switcher. Every piece of UI chrome — filter labels, tabs, buttons, tooltips, the Stats and Patent Fights view copy, the footer, and the Colorado MTB Innovation panel — is fully translated. **The French text for the patent write-ups themselves (titles, summaries, and "why it matters" analysis) is AI-drafted and has not yet been checked by a French speaker against the English original** — a banner says so on-page for as long as that's true, and stays until a real review happens. This is a deliberate, disclosed tradeoff, not an oversight: patent-law and suspension-engineering terminology is exactly the kind of text where a wrong word is worse than no word, so it's flagged rather than presented as finished. All 276 entries have a drafted French version as of the latest pass — the 7 that briefly rendered English-only under French (added to `main` while the initial translation branch was in flight: the three absoluteBLACK EU design entries, the two WickWerks/RampWerks chainring patents, the RampWerks v. absoluteBLACK litigation entry, and the Shimano Ice Tech rotor) were caught up in a follow-up pass rather than left as a permanent gap. New entries added from here forward are expected to ship bilingual from the start; the `tx()`/`txEN()` fallback mechanism described above still exists and still renders English gracefully for any field that isn't, so a future gap stays an honest one rather than a rendering bug. Language choice is remembered per-browser via `localStorage` (a preference only — the tool works fine with it empty), the page opens in English by default, and every deep link (`#p=...`) and Patent Fights jump-link resolves to the same card regardless of which language is active. A bare `#fr` or `#en` in the URL — e.g. `.../MTB-PATENT-ATLAS/#fr`, or the same on a page that embeds the atlas in an iframe pointed at that URL — opens directly in that language and updates the saved preference, so it works as a shareable/embeddable link, not just a same-session toggle. It combines with the other deep-link params too (`#cat=susp&lang=fr`), using `lang=fr`/`lang=en` instead of the bare form in that case.

### Scrollable filter tabs
Eight tabs across the top of the filter bar: Category · Status · Story · Inventors · Brands · All · 📊 Stats · ⚔ Fights. The Inventors and Brands rows (the long ones) have left/right scroll arrows that appear only when content overflows, soft fade-edge cues, and a "Show all" button that wraps everything onto multiple lines for full at-a-glance access.

### International patent office links
Entries with non-US filings carry a jurisdiction tag and link out to the relevant national patent office (DPMA, UK IPO, INPI, PRV, EPO/Espacenet, IP Australia, CIPO, UPRP) or to the direct Google Patents record when available. Taiwan's 3-letter TWI prefix and US reissue patents (RE-prefix) are handled automatically by the link-building logic. `j:"EU"` is a deliberately separate jurisdiction from `j:"EP"`: EPO/Espacenet covers European *patents*, while EUIPO (linked for `EU`) administers Registered Community Designs — an industrial-design registration, not a utility patent. EUIPO designs aren't indexed by Google Patents at all, so `EU` entries always carry `num:null` and cite their RCD numbers (format `NNNNNNNNN-NNNN`) in prose instead of a per-record link.

### PMR link
A "PMR ↗" button in the header top-right returns to [postmillenniumrenaissance.com](https://www.postmillenniumrenaissance.com), the parent site for this and the rest of the PMR tool suite.

## Tech stack

Single HTML file. No build step, no package manager, no framework. Six Google Fonts loaded via CDN: Barlow and Barlow Condensed for shared body/UI text, plus one display font per visual theme (Nunito, Bitter, Anton, IBM Plex Mono — see "Four distinct visual themes" above). Opens directly in a browser or deploys as-is to GitHub Pages. Tested against a jsdom harness covering filtering, theme token application, era-bracket aggregation at all three bar-chart granularities, Patent Fights rendering, Stats view rendering and click-through, going-free-soon matching, deep-link read/write (including graceful fallback when the browser blocks URL rewriting), surprise-me from every tab, Colorado panel visibility, scroll-arrow behavior, `who[]` registration integrity against `BRANDS`/`INVENTORS`, and regression across all major features after each build.

---

## Embedding in Pinkbike or similar platforms

The page works as a standalone link or an `[IFRAME]` embed. Pinkbike's embed syntax:

```
[IFRAME width=text height=1100 mobileHeight=1800 src="https://postmillennium-mtb.github.io/MTB-PATENT-ATLAS/"]
```

**Known limitation:** `position: sticky` (filter bar) and `position: fixed` (Colorado panel) resolve against the *iframe's* own viewport, not the host page. Inside a fixed-height iframe, the sticky filter bar scrolls away when the reader scrolls the article page and doesn't return without manually scrolling inside the iframe. On mobile this is the most noticeable friction point. A `?embed=1` mode that wraps the atlas in its own internal scroll container would fix this cleanly and is a planned improvement. Test in an actual iframe before publishing.

**Deep links inside an iframe:** the visible browser address bar always reflects wherever this document is actually loaded from — the standalone `github.io` URL, or the iframe's own address when embedded — never the parent page that embeds it. That's a consequence of how the hash-routing is written (this document can only rewrite its own `location` via `history.replaceState`, not a cross-origin parent's), and it's the right behavior for that mechanism specifically: it's the only URL that resolves correctly on its own without any cooperation from whatever page is embedding it. If the browser blocks URL rewriting inside a sandboxed iframe (some embed contexts do), the tool degrades gracefully: filtering and browsing still work, only the "address bar reflects current view" behavior is skipped.

**Share, copy-link, and cite-this-entry links are a separate mechanism** from the address-bar behavior above, and deliberately don't follow it: every "📋 Copy link," per-card share button, and "cite this entry" link is built against a fixed `SHARE_BASE_URL` constant (`https://postmillenniumrenaissance.com/atlas/`) rather than this document's own address, so what a reader actually copies or shares is always the on-brand postmillenniumrenaissance.com URL — never the raw `github.io` implementation detail — regardless of whether the page happens to be running standalone or embedded when they click it. This works because pmr-website's own `/atlas/index.html` already reads its own `location.hash` on load and appends it straight onto this tool's iframe `src`, so a link like `https://postmillenniumrenaissance.com/atlas/#p=maestro-suspension` opens that page, which loads the atlas into its iframe with that same hash, which this tool's own existing deep-link logic then reads exactly as it would from a bare `github.io` link. That forwarding only runs once, on that page's own initial load, so it resolves a fresh share link correctly but does not live-sync the parent page's own address bar while someone browses around inside an already-open embed — the address-bar paragraph above still applies to that case.

**These links carry the current language, too.** All three deep-link builders (`citeUrl`, the per-card share button, and `syncHash()`, which the general Share menu and the browser's own address bar both read from) append `&lang=fr` whenever `state.lang` isn't the English default — mirroring `langFromHash()`, which has been able to *read* a `#...&lang=fr` combination since the language switcher shipped, but had nothing writing one until this pass. Before this fix, sharing, citing, or copying a link while reading in French silently handed the recipient an English page — the copied citation *text* was already correctly French (`tx()` covers that), only the URL embedded in or alongside it wasn't. English is still the unmarked default (a plain `#p=slug` link, still the overwhelmingly common case, doesn't grow a redundant `&lang=en`).

## Data schema

All content lives in the `D` array near the top of the `<script>` block. Each entry is a plain JavaScript object:

```js
{
  y: 1996,                    // year filed (required)
  g: 1997,                    // year granted, or null if pending
  t: "Title (US 1,234,567)",  // put the headline patent number in the title when there is one
  a: "Assignee / inventor",   // free text, shown on the card
  num: "1234567",             // primary patent number — plain digits, no commas, no prefix
  nums: ["1234567","7654321"],// OPTIONAL: only when a real portfolio needs multiple links
  cat: "susp",                // susp | fork | drive | wheel | comp | emtb | frame | transport
  st: "active",               // active | expired | pending | unknown
  exp: 2017,                  // estimated expiry (filing year + 20), or null
  j: "DE",                    // OPTIONAL: omit entirely for US filings
  b: ["litigated"],           // licensor | acquired | givenaway | litigated | priorart
  who: ["Trek"],              // brand/inventor filter tags — must exist in BRANDS or INVENTORS
  conf: "v",                  // v = verified | m = unverified number | l = draft / in progress
  s: "Factual summary...",    // 1–3 sentences, what the patent covers and its history
  w: "Why it matters..."      // editorial payoff — what changed because of this patent
}
```

`exp` also drives the **Going free soon** filter and Stats watchlist automatically — active entries with `exp` within two years of the current year surface there with no extra tagging needed.

A single entry can also stand in for a whole product family rather than one specific filing — several GoPro entries do this deliberately (see *Bundled entries*, below) rather than adding a card per patent in a 1,600+ patent portfolio.

### Patent number formatting

Store numbers as plain digit strings with no commas, spaces, or `US` prefix. The `numLink` / `patentUrl` functions detect prefixes and format display labels automatically:

**Design patents are the one place this trips people up.** Store the number as *bare digits* and set `pt:"design"`; the rendering code prepends the `D` itself, for both the display label and the Google Patents URL (which needs `USD1140680`, not `US1140680`). Storing `"D1140680"` *and* `pt:"design"` doubles the prefix into a broken `DD1140680` link — that exact mistake has been caught here twice. The `nums` array is the exception: entries in it are format-sniffed individually by `numLink()`, so a design patent inside a `nums` list *does* carry its own `D` (see OneUp's `"D896132"`).

| Type | Store as | Displays as | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| Standard US grant | `"7334846"` | US 7,334,846 | Stan's BST |
| US design patent | `"1140680"` + `pt:"design"` | US D1,140,680 | Garrett in-axle tool |
| US reissue patent | `"RE45684"` | US RE 45,684 | Manitou |
| 2-letter country prefix | `"EP3111109"` | EP 3,111,109 | MRP Wave Ring |
| 3-letter country prefix | `"TWI386342"` | TWI 386,342 | Manitou Taiwan |
| WO publication | `"WO2025003104"` | WO 2025/003,104 | KOM Xeno Hub |
| US application | `"20190136918"` | US 20190136918 | Bill Shook ratchet ring |

### Confidence tiers

The `conf` field is an honesty mechanism, not cosmetic metadata:

- **`"v"`** — verified: patent number independently confirmed against a primary source (USPTO, Google Patents, a manufacturer's own virtual-marking page, or court filings)
- **`"m"`** — medium: the technology and story are confirmed but the specific number wasn't independently re-verified this pass
- **`"l"`** — draft: existence confirmed, number not yet located, or research still in progress

Since September 2026 the `"m"` tier renders two different labels, because it covers two different situations and showing one label for both was actively misleading — a card carrying a real patent number still read "patent number unverified", which a reader reasonably takes to mean the number itself is doubtful. `confLabel()` now branches on whether a number is actually present: **"patent number unverified"** when `num`/`nums` are empty (the number is the gap), **"number listed, not re-verified"** when a number is there (what is missing is a fresh primary-source re-check, or the entry deliberately stands in for a portfolio). This is wording only — the stored tiers are untouched.

Several entries (WRP, EXT, Forbidden, Lauf's original Grit fork, Antidote, OPEN BAR, and the bundled GoPro category entries) are intentionally tagged `"m"` or `"l"` because the underlying companies operate on a deliberate patent-pending or trade-secret strategy, the entry stands in for an entire portfolio rather than one filing — not because of incomplete research. When adding entries, prefer an honest `"m"`/`"l"` over inflating to `"v"`.

### Bundled entries

A handful of entries represent a *category* of patents rather than a single filing — currently GoPro's action-camera portfolio (image processing/FOV, stabilization, hardware/acoustics, telemetry), grouped into four cards instead of one per patent. Use this pattern when a company's IP position matters to the story but individually cataloguing a 1,000+ patent portfolio would swamp the rest of the dataset. Bundled entries cite one representative patent number where a specific filing is genuinely the best example, and are tagged `conf: "l"` since no single number fully verifies the bundle.

### Adding a new brand or inventor

Every value used in an entry's `who[]` array must already exist in `BRANDS` (companies) or `INVENTORS` (named individuals, `{label, key}` pairs) near the top of the script. If a chip doesn't appear in the filter row, the missing registration here is the first place to check.

### Adding a new Patent Fight

Add an object to the `FIGHTS` array with: `key`, `title`, `sub`, `combatants[]`, `era`, `stakes`, `outcome`, and `cards[]`. The `cards[]` array takes patent number strings or distinctive title substrings; `fightCardMatches()` scans all `D[]` entries and auto-generates tap-to-jump chips for every match.

### A note on card identity

Each rendered card gets an HTML `id` of `p-<patent number>` (or a slugified title if there's no number), used by both the deep-link and surprise-me features to jump to a specific entry. Adding a new patent doesn't require touching this — the id is generated automatically from existing fields at render time.

---

## Methodology and sourcing

Compiled from USPTO and Google Patents records, manufacturer virtual-marking pages (Fox, Wolf Tooth, Hayes Bicycle Group, MRP, Shimano, Manitou, and others), court filings, and industry reporting (Pinkbike, BikeRadar, Bicycle Retailer, Singletracks, MTBR, Singletrack World, Vital MTB, Escape Collective).

Primary-source verification is preferred throughout. Where verification wasn't possible, entries carry an honest confidence label rather than inflated certainty. This includes checking third-party product claims against the primary patent record rather than taking them at face value — a product marketed under a "chin mount" name, for instance, doesn't necessarily mean the seller holds a patent on the mechanism, or that a patent with a similar-sounding title is actually the same invention.

Expiration dates are estimated as filing year + 20 years and do not account for terminal disclaimers, maintenance fee lapses, or patent term adjustments. **Nothing here is legal advice.**

For patents that issued before June 8, 1995 (when US law switched to a 20-years-from-filing term), `exp` is instead estimated as **grant year + 17 years** for utility patents or **grant year + 14 years** for design patents, matching the actual pre-GATT statutory term. The atlas's three Schwinn entries (1938–1966) are the only current cases old enough for this to matter, but it's the correct rule for any pre-1995 filing added later.

Corrections, additional patents, and better sourcing are welcome — open an issue or PR.

---

## Recent updates (September 2026)

- **Added a language switcher: English / French.** EN/FR buttons sit next to the theme switcher in the header. All UI chrome (filter labels, buttons, tooltips, the Stats and Patent Fights view copy, the footer, and the Colorado MTB Innovation panel) is translated and wired through a `T[lang]` registry, with helper accessors (`t()` for chrome strings, `tx()` for data fields) that fall back to English if a translation is ever missing. Every `t`/`s`/`w` field — plus image alt text and captions — across all 256 patent entries, and the `title`/`sub`/`stakes`/`outcome` fields on all 7 Patent Fights, was converted from a plain string to a `{en, fr}` object. Deep links (`#p=...`) and Patent Fights → card jump-links use a stable, English-derived slug so they keep working regardless of which language is active when a link is generated or opened; free-text search checks both languages at once so switching languages mid-session never breaks a query already typed. Language choice persists via `localStorage` (a preference only — same pattern as the theme switcher, the tool works fine with it empty) and the page defaults to English.
- **The French translation of the 256 entries is AI-drafted and unreviewed.** A banner says so on-page whenever French is selected (dismissible for that page view, but it comes back on reload or re-selecting French, rather than being permanently silenced) and stays until a French speaker has actually checked the technical/legal terminology against the English source — patent-law and suspension-engineering vocabulary is exactly the kind of text where a wrong word is worse than no word. Treat the French data as a first draft, not a verified translation, until that review happens.
- **Scope note for future language additions:** the `T` registry and the `{en, fr}` field pattern are built to extend to more languages (add a language button, a `T.xx` chrome block, and an `xx` key on each `{en, fr}` data field) without another structural pass — but no German, Spanish, or other-language content has been drafted yet.
- **Reconciled with four entries added to `main` after the translation pass.** The three absoluteBLACK EU design entries, the two WickWerks/RampWerks chainring patents plus the RampWerks v. absoluteBLACK litigation entry (and its new Patent Fight), and the Shimano Ice Tech rotor entry all landed on `main` independently while this branch's translation work was in progress. None were retranslated here — they carry through as plain English strings and render correctly under either language via the same `tx()`/`txEN()` fallback that already covers any not-yet-translated field, consistent with the gap called out above. `NATOFFICE.EU`'s label was converted to a `{en, fr}` pair to match the rest of that registry (a plain string there would have broken `natOfficeFull()`'s language lookup), and the `shareText` patent count was bumped 256 → 263 in both languages to match the new total.
- **Added `#fr`/`#en` as direct-load language links.** A bare `#fr` or `#en` at the end of the URL (e.g. `.../MTB-PATENT-ATLAS/#fr`) now opens the page straight into that language and updates the remembered `localStorage` preference, the same as clicking the toggle — meant for a link or iframe `src` on postmillenniumrenaissance.com that should always land in French, without waiting for a click. Combines with the existing filter/search deep-link params via `lang=fr`/`lang=en` (`#cat=susp&lang=fr`) rather than the bare form. Read once at initial page load, same as every other deep-link param — it doesn't react to the hash changing after the page is already open, which matches how `#p=...`/`#cat=...` already behave.
- **Fixed the social-preview card.** `og:image` was pointing at `apple-touch-icon.png`, a 180×180 app icon, and `twitter:card` was set to `summary` (small square) with no dedicated `twitter:image` — so any share of this page's own URL rendered a tiny, badly-cropped icon rather than a real preview card. Added `social-preview.png` (1000×852, the same artwork already used as this tool's card thumbnail on the postmillenniumrenaissance.com homepage) as a self-contained asset in this repo, wired it into `og:image`/`twitter:image` with correct `og:image:width`/`og:image:height`, and switched `twitter:card` to `summary_large_image`. Also synced `twitter:description` to match `og:description` word-for-word — they'd drifted into two different summaries of the same page. Not pixel-perfect for the platform-ideal 1200×630 ratio (this asset will get center-cropped slightly on some platforms), but a large, correct, on-topic image now beats a mis-sized icon.
- **Added a "Patents by category, over time" stacked bar chart to the Stats tab.** Fixed 5-year brackets (independent of the top scrubber's 1/2/5yr toggle — that chart answers "how much," this one answers "what kind"), stacked by the eight existing categories, with a per-segment hover tooltip and a legend underneath. The eight category colors (`CAT_COLORS`) are a categorical palette validated for colorblind-safe adjacent-pair separation against both light chart surfaces this tool uses (`#FFFFFF` and the COMBA theme's cream), rather than picked by eye — one adjacent pair (yellow/aqua) sits in the acceptable-with-relief band, mitigated by the always-visible legend and hover-tooltip counts. Bilingual (`catTimelineTitle`/`catTimelineIntro` in the `T` registry) and responsive down to the mobile breakpoint. The underlying era-bucketing logic (`getEras()`) was refactored into a parameterized `buildEras(step)` so the new chart's fixed 5-year buckets and the top scrubber's adjustable step share one implementation instead of two copies that could drift apart — spot-checked visually afterward to confirm the original top-of-page chart still renders identically. Visual QA was done via Playwright screenshots of the static file (desktop, mobile width, and the COMBA theme in French); there is still no automated visual regression check, per the project's "no committed test suite" convention.
- **Changed that chart's grid to start at 1980.** The regular 5-year buckets used to span the full dataset back to 1884 — mostly empty grid, and the handful of real pre-1980 entries too sparse for an 8-way category stack to read as anything but noise. The 16 entries filed before 1980 aren't dropped: they're folded into one leading "**\<1980**" bar (its own hover tooltip and always-visible axis label, bilingual: "Before 1980"/"Avant 1980"), so the chart's total still matches `D.length` — none of the atlas's oldest entries quietly vanish from their own chart. New `CATEGORY_TIMELINE_START_YR` constant; `catTimelineHTML()` restructured to build a "buckets" list (the regular per-`buildEras()` brackets plus the synthetic leading one) rather than mapping `buildEras()`'s output directly. Re-verified visually on desktop, mobile, and French after the change.
- **Moved "Going free soon" to the bottom of the Stats tab**, below Most-patented names, instead of right after the status-split bar — a pure markup reorder inside `renderStats()`, no logic or data changed.
- **Clarified that the Most-patented names leaderboard counts atlas entries, not real-world patent holdings.** Its intro text (both languages) now says so explicitly, naming GoPro, SRAM, Shimano, and Fox as brands whose actual portfolios run far larger than what's individually cataloged here — flagged after a spot-check noted GoPro's 7-entry bar reads, without that context, as if GoPro held only 7 patents total, when the atlas's own GoPro FOV entry already cites "1,600+ granted patents globally, 1,000+ in the US" for that one company alone. Same caveat added to this README's Stats view description. No entry data or counts changed — this is a labeling fix, not a re-verification of any brand's real patent totals.
- **Added a ninth category: Cameras & Wearables (`tech`).** Resolves the "Tech category question" this file had flagged as open since July — see *A note on the "Tech" category question* (below) for the full before/after and the scope test applied. All 7 GoPro-family entries (image processing/FOV, stabilization, hardware/acoustics, Forcite electronics, multi-camera sync, GPS telemetry, chin mount) moved from `comp`/`emtb` into `tech`; nothing else did — Di2, AXS, Flight Attendant, and every other electronically-actuated bike system stayed in its functional category, since the test is "does this control the bike itself," not "is this electronic." Filter chips, card pills, and the "Entries by category" bar all picked up the new category with zero code changes (`CATS` is fully data-driven, as this file's own registry table already documented). `CAT_COLORS` gained a `tech` entry too, but deliberately as a neutral gray "Other"-style fill rather than a 9th competing hue — the categorical palette this atlas follows is structurally capped at 8 hues by design.
- **Added a Count/Share toggle to "Patents by category, over time."** Count (the existing default) scales each bar to that bracket's total volume. Share holds every bar at full height so only the category *mix* varies — useful for comparing composition across eras (e.g. rear-suspension's early dominance giving way to newer categories) without a quiet era's bar being too short to read next to a busy one. Toggled with a small button in the chart's own header; state doesn't persist across a reload, matching this chart's existing behavior (unlike the theme/language toggles, which do).
- **Added "Litigation over time" to the Stats tab**, right below the category chart. Same 5-year brackets (now shared via a renamed `STATS_TIMELINE_STEP_YR`/`STATS_TIMELINE_START_YR`, since both charts genuinely want identical eras — `CATEGORY_TIMELINE_*` was renamed rather than duplicated), split litigated (in the same red used everywhere else in the tool for that badge) vs. not. Answers whether the recent-looking cluster of litigated entries in the dataset is a real trend or just recency bias in what got reported and documented. Refactored the chart-rendering internals so both "over time" charts share one `buildStatsTimelineBuckets()` (bucket assembly) and one `renderStatsTimelineChart()` (column/label/legend rendering) rather than duplicating either — the category chart's own `CAT_COLORS`/`catLabel()` and the litigation chart's 2-color/2-label scheme just feed different data into the same renderer. CSS classes generalized to match (`.cat-timeline` → `.stats-timeline`, etc.) since the shape is now shared by two charts, not one.
- **Added a data export menu to the Stats tab.** "⬇ Export data" opens a small menu (reusing the Share button's `<details>` pattern) offering the full dataset as JSON (complete `{en,fr}` structure, full fidelity) or as a flattened, English-language CSV (spreadsheet-friendly; bilingual fields reduced to their English text, array fields like `who`/`b` joined with `; `). An advocacy-tool credibility feature — lets a reader verify or reuse the raw data without scraping the page. Standard client-side Blob download, no server and no new dependency; noted honestly in the code comment that a strictly `sandbox`-ed embedding iframe without `allow-downloads` could degrade this to "view the raw text" rather than "save the file," since this tool is designed to run in exactly that kind of iframe.
- **Added a "cite this entry" button to every card**, next to the existing copy-link button. Copies a plain-text citation (patent number, title, filed/granted years, assignee, and a permalink to the card) to the clipboard — meant for readers who cite specific patents in their own writing. Honest about entries with no confirmed number ("patent number not yet confirmed") rather than guessing one, matching this dataset's sourcing rules. The two per-card icon buttons now share one `.card-icon-btn` CSS base instead of duplicating the circular-button styling per button.
- **Added the two foundational penny-farthing/"ordinary" bicycle patents**, pushing the atlas's own year range back from 1884 to 1869 and giving the Wheels & Tires category its historical root. Eugène Meyer's wire-spoke tension wheel (French patent No. 86,705, August 4, 1869) is the invention that first made a large, lightweight front wheel structurally practical; James Starley and William Hillman's lever-tension wheel and driving-gear patent (British patent No. 2236, taken out August 11, 1870) turned it into the commercial "Ariel" — the machine the penny-farthing silhouette is named for. Both tier `"v"` — cross-confirmed across multiple independent search results (including a ResearchGate academic figure caption for the French number), but **the primary-source pages themselves (Wikipedia, the Online Bicycle Museum, Graces Guide, ResearchGate, Britannica, UDC Penny Farthing) were all blocked by this session's network egress proxy**, so nothing here was verified by directly reading the source — a future session with working access should double-check both numbers/dates against a primary record before treating this as fully closed. The British patent is `num: null` on purpose, not by omission: pre-1916 UK patents were numbered sequentially *within each year*, so a bare "2236" would auto-link to a real but completely unrelated modern GB patent under this atlas's existing link-building logic — a new, documented exception in CLAUDE.md (same shape as the existing EU RCD one), with the actual year-qualified number cited in the entry's own prose instead. Registered two new inventors (Eugène Meyer, James Starley); William Hillman is credited in the entry's own text but not given a separate filter chip, matching this atlas's existing precedent for an asymmetrically-credited co-inventor. Also fixed a pre-existing bug spotted while touching this same historical cluster: the 1890 belt-driven-bicycle entry's `exp` was computed as grant+20 (the modern post-GATT rule) instead of grant+17 (the correct pre-1995 rule for a patent this old) — corrected 1910 → 1907.
- **Added two genuinely non-US-numbered entries: myStromer/Fairly Bike's frame-integrated e-bike motor hub patent, and the resulting myStromer v. Revolt Zycling litigation.** Requested specifically as a "how global is this atlas" pass — most well-documented foreign-company inventions turn out to be filed and discussed as *US* patents (WebSearch itself is US-only per its own tool description, which structurally biases what surfaces), so after multiple unsuccessful searches for real, attributable non-US patent numbers from Magura, Syntace, Mondraker, KindShock, Acros, Trickstuff, and Formula (all real companies, none with a specific number this pass could confidently confirm and tie to a documented product/story), this EU-numbered pair was the one that actually cleared the bar. EP 2,546,134 B1 ("Combination structure of a bicycle frame and a motor hub," filed Oct. 11, 2011, granted March 25, 2015) is jointly held by Switzerland's myStromer AG and Taiwan's Fairly Bike Manufacturing; myStromer's 2023 suit against fellow Swiss maker Revolt Zycling (UPC_CFI_177/2023, Düsseldorf local division) produced the brand-new Unified Patent Court's first-ever ex parte preliminary injunction, days after the UPC itself began operating, and settled in March 2024 with a sales ban on Revolt's Opium e-bikes across five countries. Cross-confirmed across multiple independent IP-law sources (JUVE Patent, Finnegan, IPKat, Carpmaels & Ransford, Casalonga, Bicycle Retailer, SGB Media) — tiered `"v"`. Flagged honestly as an edge-of-scope inclusion, same call as the existing Mavic road-e-bike and Forcite motorcycle-helmet entries: myStromer builds fast commuter S-pedelecs, not off-road mountain bikes, but the frame/motor-hub integration approach and the landmark legal precedent are both directly relevant to e-MTB design and to how any future European e-bike patent dispute might play out. Registered `myStromer` and `Revolt Zycling` in `BRANDS`; `Fairly Bike Manufacturing` is credited in the entry's own text only, matching the same asymmetric-co-credit precedent as the Hillman/Starley entry above.
- **Added the Rut Rack no-touch hitch bike carrier (patent pending), the atlas's newest pending application.** Surfaced from a Singletracks writeup: inventor Wayne Lockwood filed a utility application on June 4, 2025 (independently confirmed twice), still under USPTO examination, for a hitch rack with zero moving parts or straps that instead relies on friction and an unusually low resting center of gravity for stability. `num: null` on purpose, not by omission — no application/publication number is public yet, and won't be until roughly 18 months post-filing (~early 2027) unless early publication was requested. Tiered `"m"`: the story, filer, and filing date are concretely sourced to a named article, but there's no number to independently verify. Registered `Rut Rack` in `BRANDS`; Lockwood himself wasn't added to `INVENTORS` since this is his only entry so far, matching the existing "recurring figure only" rule for that registry. **A related lead investigated but deliberately not added:** RatioX (Lausanne, Switzerland — a mechanical CVT bicycle gearbox, founder Simon Faneco) looked like a strong candidate, with a US patent (11,208,175, "Continuously variable transmission for a bicycle") and a WO/PCT publication (WO2017103110) both independently cross-confirmed by title and number across separate search queries. It was left out of `D` anyway: one further search pass, meant to confirm the inventor/assignee, returned an internally inconsistent claim (a different inventor count and an unrelated "beverage makers" patent attributed to the same name) that could not be reconciled — a clear case of the search tool's own AI-generated summary text being unreliable rather than a fact about the patent itself, and direct reads of both Google Patents and RatioX's own site were blocked to this session throughout. Rather than guess which version was real, RatioX is named here as a confirmed-real, not-yet-verifiable-enough candidate for a future pass with working fetch access or a user-supplied primary-source excerpt (the approach that would resolve it fastest, per this repo's known network constraint).
- **Added a faint carpet texture to the header banner**, replacing the flat `--header-bg` fill with the same USD28,278 (Crowe, 1898, expired design patent) weave used as the page-wide background on postmillenniumrenaissance.com. Implemented as a `header::before` pseudo-element masked with `images/pmr-carpet-1898-mask.png` (copied into this repo from the main site's `images/` folder, with its derivation notes in `images/pmr-carpet-1898-SOURCE.md`), tinted white at 8% opacity since `--header-bg` is dark in all four themes, tiled at a smaller 260px repeat to suit the shorter header band. Purely visual — no data, schema, or JS logic touched. Unverified visually beyond a code read, per this project's "no browser rendering check available" convention; a session with screenshot access should confirm it reads correctly across all four themes before calling it final.
- **Added Hugo Auguste Becker's 1890 spring-suspended velocipede frame (US 439,095) — the oldest entry in the suspension category, 104 years ahead of the next-earliest (Horst Leitner's 1994 Horst Link).** The user supplied both sheets of the actual patent drawings directly (`pictures/US439095-becker-page-1.png` and `-page-2.png`), which is as close to a primary source as this dataset gets — number, title ("Velocipede"), inventor's own signature, and the Oct. 28, 1890 grant date are all legible straight off the document, independently cross-confirmed by a Google Patents search hit matching the same number, title, and inventor name. Filed Oct. 19, 1889, this predates the post-GATT rule, so `exp` follows the pre-1995 formula (grant + 17 = 1907), not the modern filing + 20 used almost everywhere else in this atlas. The design hinges the frame into front and rear sections, each cushioned by its own coil spring at the joint — cushioning the whole frame rather than isolating wheel travel, a genuinely different approach from every later entry in this category. Tiered `"v"`. Sheet 1 (Fig. 1, the full velocipede) is used as the entry's `img`; sheet 2 (Figs. 2–3, the close-up spring-joint mechanism) is real and sourced but unused for now — this schema supports one image per entry, so it sits in `pictures/` as a candidate for a future multi-image feature rather than being discarded. One thing not independently confirmed this pass: search-summary text named a specific hometown for Becker, but given this session's own recent, direct experience with that summarizer fabricating a detail on an unrelated patent (see the RatioX note above), that claim wasn't trusted enough to repeat here — the entry states only what the primary document and a separate, matching search result actually support. Registered `Hugo Auguste Becker` in `INVENTORS`, following the same one-off-historical-figure precedent as Eugène Meyer and James Starley.
- **Swapped the header texture to a second Eugene A. Crowe carpet design: US Design Patent 25,495** ("Carpet," patented May 12, 1896, also assigned to the E. S. Higgins Carpet Company — a sibling to USD28,278, the 1898 pattern this replaces in the header and that stays in use as the page-wide texture on postmillenniumrenaissance.com). Same public-domain basis as its sibling (an 1890s design-patent term of at most 14 years, well over a century expired). Jon supplied the plate image directly rather than it being fetched from the PDF link this pass — see `images/pmr-carpet-1896-SOURCE.md` for the full derivation (content-bbox crop, Lanczos downscale to 800px wide, Otsu threshold with a soft ramp into a transparent alpha mask) and the honest caveat on it: the patent number/date/assignee were read off the OCR text supplied alongside the image, not independently re-confirmed against the source PDF, since that host is one of this session's blocked egress targets. Same `header::before` mechanism as before (white at 8% opacity, 260px tile) — only the mask file changed. Visually confirmed via Playwright screenshot across all four themes before shipping.
- **Fixed a visible gap between header tiles, spotted live on postmillenniumrenaissance.com after the swap above shipped.** The initial 1896 mask crop kept a blank-paper margin (ink density fading toward the outer ~20px on the left/right edges) that the local screenshot check hadn't caught, because at small scale it just looked like faint background rather than a seam — full-page production tiling made it obvious. Re-cropped inward past the fade band (see `images/pmr-carpet-1896-SOURCE.md` for the density-profile method and the corrected crop bounds) and re-verified with a 3×3 tile test plus a fresh four-theme screenshot before re-shipping. Same files, same wiring — only the crop changed.
- **Added breathing room between the language row and the `<h1>` title in the header**, flagged as feeling crowded under the CAMBA theme in particular (its heavier, uppercase display font sits closer to the language row than the other three themes' lighter faces). Added `margin-top:14px` to the shared `h1` rule rather than touching `.lang-row`'s own spacing, since that class isn't reused elsewhere on the page. Visually confirmed across all four themes.
- **Moved the footer's squirrel flourish from inline (sitting under the "/" in "RedFoxRun / PostMillennium MTB project.") to its own centered-over-left-margin block directly above that credit line.** The squirrel is now a standalone `<svg class="footer-squirrel">` rather than nested inside a `.footer-slash` wrapper span, and the `footerCredit` chrome string (both languages) went back to plain text since the icon no longer lives inside it. Visually confirmed across all four themes and at mobile width.
- **Added drawing images to the existing Split Pivot entry (US 7,717,212).** The user also supplied `pictures/US7717212B2.png` (Fig. 8, the concentric-pivot bearing/seal stack) and `US7717212B2_b.png` (Fig. 4, the full-bike linkage view) for Dave Weagle's Split Pivot patent, which had no image despite its direct rival, Trek's ABP (US 7,837,213), already carrying one. Used Fig. 8 as the entry's `img` — it's the direct visual counterpart to ABP's own Fig. 5 bearing-stack drawing a few lines down, letting a reader compare the two patents' hardware side by side, and the entry's `w` text now points that comparison out explicitly. Fig. 4 (the full-bike view) is real and sourced but unused for the same one-image-per-entry reason as the Becker page 2 above.
- **Counts synced**: README At-a-glance table (272→273 total, 90→91 expired, 52→53 named inventors, 151→152 verified) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings (three meta description tags, EN/FR `shareText`, EN/FR `mtBannerText`, the CSS-comment "N-entry data block" note).
- **Share, copy-link, and cite-this-entry links now point at `postmillenniumrenaissance.com/atlas/` instead of the raw `github.io` URL.** Requested directly: the reader-facing links this tool hands out should look like the product's own address, not its GitHub Pages implementation detail. All four call sites (`cardHTML()`'s `citeUrl`, the per-card share button, and the general Share menu's copy/X/Facebook/Reddit options) now build off a new `SHARE_BASE_URL` constant (`https://postmillenniumrenaissance.com/atlas/`, bare domain to match `og:url` on every other page across pmr-website) instead of `location.origin`/`location.href`, which reflected wherever this document actually loaded from rather than its public address. This works with zero changes needed on the pmr-website side: `atlas/index.html` there already reads its own `location.hash` on load and appends it onto this tool's iframe `src`, so `https://postmillenniumrenaissance.com/atlas/#p=maestro-suspension` already deep-links correctly today, the same way a bare `github.io` link always has — confirmed by reading that file directly rather than assuming. Two URL shapes proposed in the request (`/atlas/maestro`, and a bare `#maestro` without the `p=` prefix) were considered and not used: a path-only form needs real server-side routing this static, no-build GitHub Pages site doesn't have (would mean pre-generating a real file per patent slug, a maintenance burden every future entry would inherit), and a bare-slug hash needs a second, parallel parsing branch alongside the existing structured `#p=`/`#cat=`/`#q=` scheme this tool's `HASH_SETS`-based router already handles — both flagged as the kind of restructuring this file's own conventions say to raise before doing, not fold into a same-pass link-format change. The existing internal address-bar-sync behavior (`syncHash()`/`history.replaceState`, used for the browser back button and bookmarking while standalone) is untouched — it still reflects wherever this document is actually running, which is a different, narrower job than the public share link now does; both are now documented separately under *Embedding in Pinkbike or similar platforms* above, where they used to be one conflated paragraph.
- **Fixed: share/copy/cite links now carry the current language.** Asked directly whether French "works well" for the share/copy setup added in the previous pass — it didn't, fully: `langFromHash()` has been able to read a `#...&lang=fr` combination since the language switcher shipped, but nothing on the write side ever set one, so a card shared, cited, or copied while reading in French silently handed the recipient an English page. (The citation *text* itself was already correctly French — `tx()` already covered that — only the accompanying URL wasn't.) Added a small `withLang()` helper (`cardHTML()`'s `citeUrl` and the per-card share button both now call it) and one line in `syncHash()` (`if(state.lang!=="en") p.set("lang",state.lang)`), which also fixes the general Share menu for free, since it builds its link from `location.hash` right after calling `syncHash()`. Also added a `syncHash()` call to the language-toggle button's own click handler, a related gap this same check turned up: switching languages didn't update the address bar's own hash until some unrelated action (like opening the Share menu) happened to trigger it next — bookmarking immediately after switching language would have silently kept the old language. English stays the unmarked default; a plain `#p=slug` link — still the overwhelmingly common case — doesn't grow a redundant `&lang=en`. Verified the exact output strings (with a small Node harness reproducing `withLang()`/`syncHash()`'s param-building logic, not just eyeballing the diff) for FR-with-filters, FR-with-no-other-state, and EN, confirming each parses correctly against the existing `langFromHash()`/`initFromHash()` readers and that plain English browsing is byte-for-byte unchanged.
- **Added Greg Matyas / Fatback Bikes' 170/177×12 and 190/197×12 symmetric fat-bike hub spacing — this atlas's first entry that's honestly "no patent" from the start rather than "number not yet found."** Requested directly, with a link to the author's own Pinkbike profile of Matyas ("From Anchorage, With Love"). Matyas founded Fatback Bikes (Anchorage, AK) in 2007 to build the first fat bike engineered around a truly symmetric rear triangle rather than the bent/offset frames other early fat-bike makers used; specifying a symmetric 170mm rear hub in 2008 (formalized alongside its 177mm 12mm-thru-axle counterpart) let the wheel build dish-free, and the width was adopted industry-wide as the fat-bike standard. Fatback repeated the move in 2012 at 190mm QR / 197mm TA to clear 5″ tires, which became that segment's standard in turn. A USPTO inventor search for Matyas turned up nothing — this reads as a dimension becoming an industry standard through first-to-ship adoption, not IP protection, the same pattern this atlas's existing Banshee KS-Link entry documents from a suspension angle (that entry's own title carries the same explicit "(no patent)" flag, the precedent followed here). **This session's network access could not open either the Pinkbike profile or fat-bike.com's independent "Father of the Modern Alaskan Fat-Bike" profile directly** (both domains are blocked to this sandbox) — dates and attribution are cross-confirmed across multiple independent search results instead, the same standard this repo's CLAUDE.md sets for a blocked-fetch situation with multiple agreeing sources, and shipped at `conf:"v"` on that basis, same call as the Shimano Ice Tech rotor entry earlier in this same changelog. A direct read of the Pinkbike piece by a future session (or a pasted excerpt) would still be worth doing, since it's likely the most detailed single source and wasn't available this pass. Registered `Greg Matyas` in `INVENTORS` and `Fatback` in `BRANDS`/`BRAND_HQ` (Alaska). Category `wheel`, badge `priorart`, status `unknown` (no patent, so no patent status applies) — placed at the end of the wheel category's hub cluster, after Hadley Racing. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (273→274 total, 152→153 verified, 151→152 brands, 53→54 named inventors) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings (three meta tags, EN/FR `shareText`, EN/FR `mtBannerText`, the CSS-comment entry-count note).
- **Corrected and enriched the Matyas entry above from the actual primary source.** The user supplied the Pinkbike article itself as a PDF, which this session could not fetch live — reading it directly turned up a real timeline error in the first pass and a lot of detail worth adding, so the entry was rewritten rather than patched. **Corrected:** 177mm thru-axle did not ship alongside 170mm in 2008 — it followed around 2013, after mountain bikes generally shifted to thru-axles (the 2014 Salsa Beargrease among the first fat bikes to use it); similarly, 190mm QR (2012) and 197mm thru-axle are two different years, not one — Matyas introduced 197mm on his own carbon Corvus frame in 2013, a full year before the wider industry (Borealis's Echo and updated Yampa) standardized on it in 2014. Also moved the entry's headline year to 2007 (when Matyas commissioned Phil Wood for the first true 170mm symmetric hubs) rather than 2008 (when Fatback's own Taiwan-made hubs shipped), matching this atlas's existing "date the origin, not the mass-production run" convention. **Added:** the actual carpenter's-arithmetic origin of 170mm (135mm Pugsley hub + 17.5mm offset × 2 = 170 — restoring symmetry, not inventing a number), the 3.5mm-end-cap mechanism that makes 170↔177 and 190↔197 backward-compatible by swapping caps rather than needing a different hub, a direct comparison to real measured hub asymmetry (Hope Pro 4 Fatsno: 55mm/47mm, not symmetric; Onyx Fat MFU: 47.42mm/47.42mm, symmetric), a quantified wheel-strength claim from the article's own modeling (a moderately asymmetric hub at equal 52mm flange width came out 39.67% weaker before spokes buckle, via Matt Ford's Bicycle Wheel Simulator), the article's own explicit contrast with Trek's 148x12 "Boost" hub (2014/2015) as the one modern hub standard that broke backward compatibility and shipped without published test data, John Lackey's 2015 Iditarod Trail Invitational 350-mile record on a first-gen 197mm Corvus, and the 2021 Fatback → Corvus Cycles rebrand (registered as a new `BRANDS`/`BRAND_HQ` entry, added to `who[]`). **Also added, per direct request:** a citation link to the source article itself ("From Anchorage, With Love: the Greg Matyas story," Pinkbike) at the end of the card's why-it-matters text, the same inline-link pattern already used elsewhere in this atlas (e.g. the CBF and i-Track Suspension entries' own explainer links). Still `conf:"v"` — if anything, more solidly so now: the underlying source is the author's own reporting, with Matyas's direct input credited by name, the same caliber of primary source as the sibling USA bike-parks repo's Bluewood correction. Counts unaffected (brands: 152→153 for Corvus Cycles; total, verified, and named-inventor counts unchanged from the pass above).
- **Added a small squirrel mark under the footer credit line's slash.** Requested directly: a Lucide "squirrel" icon sitting right below the "/" in "A RedFoxRun / PostMillennium MTB project." The slash lives in its own relatively-positioned inline-block span with the icon absolutely positioned under it, so it tracks the slash's own rendered position (not the line's) and stays correctly placed even if the footer text wraps onto two lines on a narrow viewport. `footerCredit` switched from `data-i18n` (textContent) to `data-i18n-html` (innerHTML), matching the existing `footerAbout` convention right above it, so the wrapped-slash markup survives a language switch instead of getting wiped by the generic text-only relabeling handler.
- **Replaced several emoji with inline Lucide SVG icons, reworded "Going free soon" to "Expiring soon," and densified the litigation-over-time chart's date labels — all requested directly, icon by icon.** Surprise Me (🎲 → dice), Share (🔗 → link), the Fights tab (⚔ → swords), and the Stats tab (📊 → chart-no-axes-combined) each swapped their emoji for a small inline SVG (`ICON_DICE`/`ICON_LINK`/`ICON_SWORDS`/`ICON_CHART`, declared once in a new ICONS section rather than duplicated into both `T.en`/`T.fr`, since none of them carry translatable text). The "Going free soon" status filter chip and the Stats tab's matching watchlist heading are now "Expiring soon" ("Expire bientôt" in French, already the exact phrase this atlas uses elsewhere for the same concept via `expiringNow`) — the chip got the hourglass-cog icon, the heading got the plain hourglass, per two separate requests. **A real gotcha this surfaced and fixed along the way:** for any icon sitting inside an element with `data-i18n` (the status chip, the Surprise Me/Share buttons, the Fights/Stats tab labels), baking the icon straight into the T-registry string would have gotten wiped to literal `<svg>...` text on the next language switch, since `applyStaticI18n()`'s generic handler does `el.textContent=`, not `innerHTML=` — fixed by keeping the icon as a sibling element outside the translated span (or, for the status chip specifically, moving `data-i18n` onto a new inner span) rather than converting more elements to `data-i18n-html`. The Stats tab's own "Expiring soon" heading didn't need this treatment — that whole section is rebuilt fresh via `innerHTML=` on every render anyway, so the icon is interpolated directly into its template. Separately, the litigation-over-time chart now shows every 5-year bucket's date (`<1980, 1980, 1985, 1990, 1995, 2000, 2005...`) instead of every 10 years, via a new optional `denseLabels` parameter on the shared `renderStatsTimelineChart()` — the category chart above it keeps its original 10-year spacing, since only the litigation chart's own call site opts in. Narrow/mobile viewports still thin to every 10 years either way (unchanged from before), so the denser labels don't crowd a 390px screen. **Also disabled, not removed:** the Stats tab's "Export data" menu is now hidden (`hidden` attribute, plus a small `.share-menu[hidden]{display:none}` CSS override — `.share-menu`'s own explicit `display:inline-block` would otherwise have silently beaten the browser's default `[hidden]` styling) per direct request to make it unavailable "for now," with its markup, click handlers, and the JSON/CSV export functions themselves completely untouched for a later re-enable — removing the `hidden` attribute alone brings it back. Verified with Playwright: every icon renders with correct text in both languages after a language switch (not wiped), the litigation chart's labels read `<1980 1980 1985 1990 1995 2000 2005 2010 2015 2020 2025` on desktop and thin to 10-year spacing on a 390px viewport with no horizontal overflow, and the export menu is hidden but still present in the DOM.
- **Added images to the ISIS Drive and Matyas entries, added an ISIS Drive site link, corrected ISIS's spline count, and de-branded the Matyas title.** All requested directly; the user supplied both images (`pictures/ISIS Drive.png`, `pictures/matyas.jpg`) via GitHub's web UI. **A real factual correction, caught by actually looking at the supplied image before writing its alt text:** the ISIS Drive entry's `s` text said "eight-spline" — the drawing itself (sheet 1 of 7, "SPINDLE," rev D) labels both cross-sections "10 PL EQUALLY SPACED" in two places, i.e. ten splines, not eight; likely an old mix-up with Shimano's 8-spline Octalink, corrected now that ISIS Drive's own published spec sheet is directly available to check against (both languages). Added the drawing as the entry's image, `imgAlt`/`caption` describing it as what it actually is (the standard's own reference drawing, marked public domain on the sheet itself) rather than defaulting to this atlas's usual "Fig. from original filing" patent-drawing caption, which would have been wrong for a document that was never a patent filing. Also added an inline link to ISIS Drive's own site (isisdrive.com/drive) at the end of the why-it-matters text, same `gp-secondary` pattern as the CBF and i-Track Suspension entries' own explainer links. For the Matyas entry: the supplied `matyas.jpg` turned out to be the exact hand-drawn sketch from page 2 of the source Pinkbike article — Surly's offset rear triangle next to Fatback's symmetric one, the literal diagram the card's `s` text already describes in prose — added as the entry's image with an accurate caption/alt rather than assuming it was a portrait photo. Also removed "Fatback" from the title's lead per direct request: `t.en` now starts "Symmetric fat-bike hub spacing..." instead of "Fatback symmetric fat-bike hub spacing...", `t.fr` similarly dropped "Fatback" (kept in French's natural mid-sentence position rather than forcing "Symétrique" to lead an awkward phrase — the point was de-branding the title, not matching English word order). Verified both images actually load (not a 404 from the space in "ISIS Drive.png"'s filename — referenced as `pictures/ISIS%20Drive.png`) via Playwright, and re-ran the D-array parse/count/bilingual-completeness script (274 total, unchanged — this pass touched existing entries' images/text, not the count).
- **Fixed: the litigation-over-time chart's dense labels silently dropped every bucket ending in 5 (1985, 1995, 2005, 2015, 2025) on a narrow render.** Reported directly after checking the live site. Root cause: on a viewport under `NARROW_BREAKPOINT_PX` (480px) — which an *iframe embed* can hit even on a normal desktop monitor, since `window.innerWidth` reflects the iframe's own width, not the visitor's screen (see this file's own "It will be iframed" non-negotiable) — the previous fix's narrow-width fallback thinned dense labels with `b.from%10===0`. Every bucket in this chart's 5-year stepping already lands on a multiple of 5, so that check is only ever true for *half* of them (the ones that are also multiples of 10); it wasn't thinning evenly, it was deleting every other real data point. Fixed by having `denseLabels` show every bucket's year unconditionally, ignoring the narrow/not-narrow distinction entirely — a 4-digit year label is short enough that all ~11 hold up fine even in a narrow embed. Needed one supporting CSS change to hold up at the very narrow end: a `.dense-labels` class (added only for this chart, not the category chart above it) drops font-size further and tightens letter-spacing at ≤480px, checked with no visual overlap down to a 320px viewport. Verified with Playwright at 1280/390/375/320px: the litigation chart now reads the full `<1980 1980 1985 1990 1995 2000 2005 2010 2015 2020 2025` sequence at every width, the category chart's own (unrelated, unchanged) thinning still applies at narrow widths, and there's no visible label overlap even at 320px.
- **Shortened both the Matyas entry's `s` (factual summary) and `w` (why-it-matters) fields, per two direct requests.** `s` cut from the full carpenter-arithmetic-through-Iditarod-record narrative to four sentences: the 2006 Pugsley's 135mm-offset compromise, the 135+(17.5×2)=170 arithmetic that fixes it, "a symmetric wheel is a strong wheel," and that Matyas founded both the 170/177x12 and 190/197x12 standards plus Fatback (rebranded Corvus Cycles, 2021). `w` cut from the full Boost-hub-contrast-and-wheel-simulator-stat paragraph to three sentences: the 170/177/190/197 progression as problem-driven engineering math, John Lackey's 2015 Iditarod Trail Invitational record on a first-gen 197mm Corvus, and a new claim (supplied directly, not independently re-verified this pass) that 197x12 remains today's fatbike standard — the Ford wheel-simulator 39.67%-weaker stat and the explicit Trek Boost-hub contrast were both dropped from the stored text; the source-article citation link was kept at the end of `w`, matching this atlas's standing citation convention, since removing it wasn't requested. Wrote fresh, correspondingly shortened `fr` translations for both fields rather than leaving the old, now-mismatched long-form French in place — same AI-drafted/unreviewed caveat as the rest of the atlas's French text applies. No count, registry, or `who[]` change; `conf:"v"` unchanged, since shortening doesn't touch what was previously verified.
- **Added the patent drawing to the Trek Y-bike entry.** The user supplied `pictures/US5685553A.png` via GitHub's web UI, already named for the entry's own patent number. It's Fig. 1 — a full side-view of the bicycle showing the Y-shaped mainframe and unified rear triangle with the drawing's original numbered reference callouts — so `imgAlt` (both languages) describes it as that specific figure rather than a generic placeholder. Ordinary case: `img`/`imgAlt` only (not the `imgs` array), since there's exactly one drawing and a real `num` already on file — the default "Fig. from original filing" caption and "View full patent →" Google Patents link both apply correctly with no per-image override needed, unlike the ISIS Drive/Matyas entries earlier in this changelog where neither default was accurate. Verified the image actually loads (naturalWidth > 0) via Playwright and re-ran the D-array count (274, unchanged).
- **Found and confirmed the Knock Block steerer-stop patent: US D845,838, a design patent on the Bontrager Knock Block-compatible stem's keyed shape.** Previously a draft placeholder (`num:null, conf:"l"`). Identified from the ~74-number list on Trek's own patents page (`trekbikes.com/us/en_US/patents/`, supplied directly by the user), by process of elimination against the page's other named product families, then cross-confirmed against real-world retail evidence (the Bontrager "Knock Block Stem" product, Wolf Tooth's Knock Block-compatible headset line) and filing-date plausibility (filed July 18, 2016, matching Knock Block's actual mid-2016 launch on the Remedy and Fuel EX; granted April 9, 2019). Two other candidates surfaced during the search and were ruled out after a second confirming query each: a shoe-cleat design patent (D844,962, unrelated) and a Canyon Bicycles steering-stop family (US 9,475,539 / US 12,466,511) that covers the same general concept but belongs to a different company. `conf` raised `"l"` → `"v"`. This pass did not find a separate Trek *utility* patent on the underlying steering-limiter concept among the numbers Trek's site lists — the design patent protects the stem's ornamental shape only, noted in the entry's own text rather than implied to be broader than it is. **A rendering bug caught before this shipped**: the first data-entry pass stored `num:"D845838"` (with the "D" already embedded) alongside `pt:"design"` — but this codebase's convention (confirmed against three other working design-patent entries) is that `pt:"design"` makes the rendering code prepend its own "D", so the two together doubled up into a broken `DD845838` Google Patents link. Caught by a Playwright smoke test before commit; fixed by storing bare digits (`num:"845838"`), matching the Shock Extension and both Crampon entries' convention. **A separate, pre-existing cosmetic quirk noticed along the way, not introduced by this fix**: the "View on Google Patents" link text renders as "US D845838" without a comma, because the shared pretty-print regex needs 7+ digits to insert one and this design number (like the existing Crampon D615,003/D667,348 entries) only has 6 — already true of those live entries too, left as-is here rather than fixed as an incidental part of an unrelated data entry.
- **Upgraded the Trek Supercaliber IsoStrut entry (US 11,242,110) from `conf:"m"` to `"v"`.** The number itself was already correct — it's listed under "Supercaliber" on Trek's own patents page (the same primary source used for the Knock Block entry above) — and an independent search turned up the patent's actual claim language (an inline shock assembly with a stanchion fitting into a seat-stay-yoke cavity in the top tube) matching the IsoStrut mechanism the entry already describes, with a grant date (Feb. 8, 2022) matching the entry's existing `g:2022`. No text or number changed, only the confidence tier.
- **Reconciled three later ABP continuation patents into the existing Active Braking Pivot entry — and corrected its filing date and status in the process.** From the same Trek patents-page list as the Knock Block/IsoStrut work above, the user flagged US 9,376,162, US 8,882,127, and US 10,106,221 as ABP-related and asked whether they belonged with the existing 2010-granted ABP entry (US 7,837,213) or needed their own. Cross-confirmed search-result text quoting the patents' own "Related U.S. Application Data" sections shows 9,376,162 as a continuation chain running Ser. No. 14/506,979 → 13/550,721 (filed Jul. 17, 2012) → 12/901,893 (filed Oct. 11, 2010) → back to the original Ser. No. 11/735,816, **filed April 16, 2007** — the same application that issued as 7,837,213 in 2010. 8,882,127's own reported priority date (2012-07-17) lines up exactly with that same intermediate application, corroborating it as a sibling continuation in the identical chain. 10,106,221 is included on weaker evidence — Trek's own site groups it with the other three under "MTB/ABP," but no search result explicitly quoted its continuation chain back to 2007 the way it did for the other two, so treat that one number as the least certain of the four. Added all four as a `nums` array on the existing entry (`num` stays `"7837213"` as the headline/litigated number). **This surfaced a real, pre-existing error**: the entry had `y:2006`/`exp:2026`/`st:"expired"`, but the confirmed non-provisional filing date is 2007, not 2006 — per this atlas's own continuation rule (a continuation inherits its parent's filing date, and only a non-provisional filing starts the 20-year clock), that moves real expiration to April 2027. Corrected to `y:2007`/`exp:2027`/`st:"active"` — as of this writing the patent is about a year from expiring, not already expired, which also means it now correctly surfaces on the "Expiring soon" watchlist. `conf` stays `"v"`: this session's patent-database access was blocked (Google Patents, Justia, USPTO's own search all unreachable), so nothing here was confirmed by directly reading a patent document, but the 2007 filing date and the two strongest continuations were each independently cross-confirmed across multiple separate search queries, meeting this repo's own bar for that situation — flagged honestly in the entry's own `w` text rather than silently upgraded.
- **Counts synced**: README At-a-glance table (153→155 verified, 65→64 medium, 56→55 draft; 132→133 active, 91→90 expired — the verified/medium/draft shift is the net effect of the Knock Block and IsoStrut confidence-tier changes above, the active/expired shift is the ABP status correction just above; total entries unchanged at 274 throughout, since none of these three changes added or removed a row).
- **Confidence-tier audit: swept all 274 entries for cards showing a real patent number while labelled "patent number unverified."** Prompted by US 11,866,114, which had its number right there in the title and on the card. 21 of 274 entries carry `conf:"m"`/`"l"` with a `num`/`nums` present. They are not one problem but three, and separating them is most of the answer:
