  - **Resolved this pass (3 upgraded to `"v"`).** **US 11,866,114** (Shimano AI suspension/dropper learning model) — the number is cross-confirmed across Pinkbike, Cycling Weekly, road.cc, notebookcheck and brujulabike, all tying that exact number to this invention; upgraded, with the *filing* date flagged in the entry as still unconfirmed, since some coverage places it in 2020, which would move expiry 2043 → 2040. **US 12,570,369 B1** (Fox Fiber Core grip) — `num` was `null`; set and verified, the grant confirmed by the eGrant document Singletracks published in full, with the filing date unreachable so `y:2026` and the 2046 expiry are both flagged provisional in the entry. **US D1,140,680** (Garrett in-axle tubeless plug tool) — confirmed directly from the USPTO Official Gazette entry the user supplied as an image, which is the primary record: "Bike Tire Repair Tool," Samuel Garrett, filed Dec. 26 2024 as application 29/980,746, term 15 years. That fixed four fields at once (`y` 2025→2024, `g` null→2026, added `pt:"design"`, `exp`→2041 as grant+15).
  - **Deliberately `"m"` and staying that way (8).** The GoPro bundles, Living Link's four-patent family, the Precision fork family, Mavic's ISM/FORE trio, Park Tool's four-jurisdiction PCS-9.3 registrations, Sotto's contract-engineered portfolio, and the BMX freecoaster entry. In these the tier is not a research gap — it records that the entry stands for a portfolio rather than for the one representative number shown. These are exactly what the new "number listed, not re-verified" label exists to describe.
  - **A genuine verification backlog (10), named here so it is a visible gap rather than a silent one.** US 12,016,421 (Forcite), US 11,019,237 (GoPro multi-camera sync), GB 2,611,590 (KOLARP), US 2011/0227312 (Turntable), US 323,289 (Blount), US 566,201 (Mackinnon & Gage), EP 4,536,547 (O-Chain), US 3,514,091 and US 4,593,923 (Park Tool), US 6,203,042 and US 8,894,083 (Trek). Each has a single specific number and is low-tiered only because no pass has re-checked it against the primary record. **This pass could not clear them:** every patent host — `patents.google.com`, `patents.justia.com`, `image-ppubs.uspto.gov`, `patentsgazette.uspto.gov`, `api.patentsview.org`, Espacenet, PATENTSCOPE, and even the Singletracks mirror of the Fox eGrant — returned 403 at the egress proxy, for `curl` and `WebFetch` alike. `WebSearch` was the only working source, so every upgrade above rests on snippet cross-confirmation or on a primary document the user supplied directly. A session with working patent-site access should work this list.

- **Corrected US 4,733,881, which had been sitting at `conf:"v"` describing the wrong invention.** The atlas catalogued it as a generic "Bicycle stunt footpeg" credited to "Freestyle BMX (1980s)" — an assignee that is not a company. It is actually *"Bicycle frame with supplemental step or platform for performing freestyle maneuvers,"* Eugene J. Szymski for **Schwinn Bicycle Company**: the step is built into the frame, formed by bowing the front of the left chainstay farther outboard than the right so the resulting shelf carries the rider's foot *and* shields the coaster-brake actuating arm from it. Caught by opening the drawing sheet the user had just uploaded and noticing it showed chainstays captioned "UNDERSIDE OF CHAINSTAY TUBES," not a peg. Fixed: title, assignee, `who:["Schwinn"]`, `cat` `comp`→`frame` (it is literally a frame patent), and `exp` 2006→2005 — filed before June 8 1995, so its term is grant+17, not filing+20. The correction history is kept in the entry's own text rather than quietly overwritten. **The wider lesson is the inverse of the one that prompted this audit:** the risk is not only a good number wearing an "unverified" label, it is a wrong entry wearing a "verified" one. `"v"` has to mean the number was checked against what the patent actually *claims*, not that the number exists. The one day-level thing still unresolved: the printed drawing sheet is dated March 29 1988 while a search snippet reported July 19 1988, so the entry states the year and says the day is uncertain.

- **Greg Matyas's 170/177 and 190/197 hub standards now carry the `givenaway` badge** alongside `priorart`, at the user's direction — the symmetric-spacing standards were worked out and released without ever being patented, which is what that badge is for. Badge total 4 → 5.

- **Wired up 13 patent drawings that were in `pictures/` but referenced by nothing.** `US10197049B2` (OneUp dropper cross-section), `US10677338B2` (WickWerks ramped-tooth chainring face-on), `US11242110B2` (Trek IsoStrut rear end), `US11866114B2` (Shimano full-bike sensor layout), `US12570369B1` (Fox grip mesh sleeve), `US4733881A` (the Schwinn chainstay sheet above), `US5632362A` (AMP hydraulic caliper exploded view), `US9102197B2` (Onyx sprag-clutch hub exploded view), `US9701361B2` (Spot Living Link leaf spring), plus second sheets for three entries that consequently move from `img` to the `imgs` array: Becker (sheet 2, the spring/hinge detail and Becker's own signature), DW-Link (`US7661503B2` Fig. 1a, the abstract kinematic diagram from sheet 1 of 56 — a much better illustration of the squat-curve method than the bike itself), and Split Pivot (`US7717212B2_b` Fig. 4, the whole-linkage view alongside the existing Fig. 8 cross-section). `D1140680.png` moved out of the repo root into `pictures/`. Every figure was opened and read before its bilingual `imgAlt` was written, so the alt text names the actual reference numerals. Entries carrying a figure: 30 → 42.

- **`US12570369B1.png` was inverted** — white-on-black, evidently captured from a dark-mode viewer, unlike all 41 other drawings. Normalised to black-on-white, autocropped and re-padded (581 KB → 230 KB).

- **Fixed a design-patent link bug that adding the Garrett figure would have triggered.** The "View full patent" link *under a figure* called `patentUrl(d.num)` directly, without the `D` prefix that `pt:"design"` entries need — so a design entry with a drawing would have linked to the same-numbered *utility* patent. A real patent, the wrong one, and wrong in the way that reads as confirmed. Latent only because no design entry had a figure until now. The number formatting is now hoisted into a single `numForUrl`/`numPretty` pair consumed by all three call sites (Google Patents button, "cite this entry", figure caption) instead of being computed twice and forgotten once — the third copy is the one that was missing. Also corrected the README's own "Patent number formatting" table, which still said design numbers are stored as `"D896132"`; the actual convention, and what the code requires, is bare digits plus `pt:"design"`.

- **Added `tools/fetch_patent_figure.py` and a `Fetch patent figure` GitHub Actions workflow**, in answer to "how could we automate adding pictures from patents?" It automates the mechanical half — download the PDF (USPTO's print endpoint first, Google Patents scrape as fallback), classify which pages are drawing sheets rather than text columns by extracted-text density, crop the page margin and the `U.S. Patent / Sheet n of m` header band, downscale, and name the output `US<number><kind>.png`. It deliberately does *not* pick which figure belongs on the card or write `imgAlt`, and never touches `index.html`: it emits several candidates plus a report containing the PDF's own front-page text, and a person finishes the job. The workflow is `workflow_dispatch`-only — Actions → Run workflow → type patent numbers → it opens a PR with the candidates — so the whole thing is usable from the GitHub web UI with nothing installed locally, and GitHub's runners have the open egress this session lacked. **Verified in parts, honestly:** the crop stage was run against five real patent sheets already in `pictures/` (US4733881A, US5632362A, US9102197B2, US7661503B2, US10197049B2) and correctly stripped headers without truncating figures, cutting file sizes by roughly half; the page-classification stage was run end-to-end against a mock six-page patent PDF and correctly picked the three drawing sheets (0 chars of text) over the front page (1,627) and the description columns (9,669). **The download stage could not be tested here at all** — both sources are 403 at this session's egress proxy — so it is written but unproven, and the script fails loudly and writes nothing rather than emitting a placeholder. Because that stage is the unproven one, it has a manual override: `--url` on the command line, or the optional `pdf_url` input in the workflow, takes the PDF link straight off the Google Patents page and skips both automatic lookups. Downloading a PDF is the step that is easy by hand and awkward to automate reliably; cropping, sizing and naming is the reverse, so the override still removes the tedious half. Note also that GitHub only exposes a `workflow_dispatch` run button once the workflow file is on the default branch, so the Actions route needs this merged to `main` first. This adds no runtime dependency to `index.html`; deleting `tools/` and the workflow file removes the feature cleanly.

- **Split the `"m"` tier's card label in two** (see Confidence tiers above): "patent number unverified" when there is no number, "number listed, not re-verified" when there is. This is the labelling half of the problem that prompted the audit — several of the 21 entries above were never really claiming their number was doubtful. Wording only, in both languages; no stored tier changed. Flagged as a user-facing wording decision, and it is a one-function revert.

- **Counts synced**: README At-a-glance table (155→158 verified, 64→62 medium, 55→54 draft — the net of the three tier upgrades above). Total entries unchanged at 274, so the six hardcoded count strings in `index.html` (three `<meta>` descriptions, `T.en`/`T.fr` `shareText`, `T.en`/`T.fr` `mtBannerText`) all still read 274 and were correctly left alone. Active 133, expired 90, pending 40, unknown 11, litigated 27 all unchanged; `givenaway` badges 4 → 5.
- **The figure tool paid for itself on its first real outing: US 3,514,091 (Park Tool repair stand clamp) is off the verification backlog, `"m"` → `"v"`, and had a wrong filing year.** Three live workflow runs were used to test the fetch stage that no sandbox could reach. The Park Tool run returned the patent's own single drawing sheet, which carries its full bibliographic header — *"CLAMPING DEVICE", filed January 13, 1967, granted May 26, 1970, J. E. Johnson et al*, with the inventors signed at the foot of the sheet as James E. Johnson, Arthur B. Engstrom and Howard C. Hawkins. The atlas had `y:1968`; the document says 1967. Corrected, `a` now credits the three named inventors alongside Park Tool, and `imgAlt` was rewritten from a generic one-liner to describe what the four figures actually show, now that the whole sheet has been read. `exp:1987` was already right (pre-GATT filing, so grant + 17). The existing `pictures/US3514091A.png` was kept rather than replaced — it is the same sheet with Google Patents' reference-numeral highlighting, which is more readable than the tool's clean render. Verified entries 158 → 159, medium 62 → 61.

- **Two bugs in the figure tool, both found by running it for real rather than by reasoning about it.** Worth recording because the first one produced *plausible-looking wrong output*, which is the dangerous kind. Measured per-page text lengths across three patents showed patent PDFs come in three shapes: a modern grant with a text layer (US 11,019,237 — page 1 bibliographic at 2,400 chars, drawings at 218–705, specification columns at ~7,500); a pure image scan with no text layer at all (US 12,016,421 — all thirteen pages extract zero characters); and a pre-1970s grant where **the order is inverted**, drawings first and specification after (US 3,514,091 — page 1 at 175 chars *is* the drawing, pages 2–5 at 5,455–8,388 are text). The classifier skipped page 1 unconditionally, which is right for the first two and exactly wrong for the third: on the Park Tool patent it discarded the only drawing in the document, then found nothing under its text threshold and fell back to "every page after the first," offering four pages of specification text as drawing candidates. In a file listing those look like perfectly good candidates. Page 1 is now dropped for being text-heavy, never for being page 1, and all three shapes are pinned as fixtures in a new `tools/test_fetch_patent_figure.py` that the workflow runs before every fetch. The second bug: a run could go green having produced nothing at all, because `figures-out/` had been added to `.gitignore` — so the PR step found nothing to commit, reported "Branch is not ahead of base", and exited 0. Removed from `.gitignore` with a comment saying why, plus a guard step that fails the run if no candidate images exist or if the directory is ignored.

- **The fetch stage works, and the fallback is what makes it work.** This was the one part of the tool that no session could test, since every patent host is 403 behind the egress proxy. Across three live runs it fetched every patent successfully — but via the Google Patents fallback on `patentimages.storage.googleapis.com`, not the USPTO print endpoint tried first. The two-source design is load-bearing, not belt-and-braces. A worry that did **not** materialise: Google served a GitHub Actions datacenter IP without complaint.

- **Opening the PR needs a repository setting the workflow cannot grant itself.** Runs log `GitHub Actions is not permitted to create or approve pull requests` unless **Settings → Actions → General → Workflow permissions → "Allow GitHub Actions to create and approve pull requests"** is ticked. Rather than let that waste a run, candidates are now uploaded as a workflow **artifact** before the PR is attempted — artifacts need no permissions — the branch `patent-figures/<run id>` is pushed regardless, and a final step prints either the PR link or exactly where the candidates are and which setting to change. Also: an empty report now says **NO TEXT LAYER** explicitly instead of sitting there blank, because a blank report reads as "nothing to check" when it means "nothing could be extracted" — a meaningful difference when the report exists to set a `conf` tier.
- **Harvested the test runs' output instead of deleting it, which cleared a second backlog entry and added two figures.** The three live workflow runs left candidate images sitting on scratch branches; rather than bin them, the useful ones were reviewed and used. **US 11,019,237 (GoPro multi-camera wireless sync) moves `"m"` → `"v"`.** Its report carried a readable front page — application 16/884,838, filed May 27 2020, granted May 25 2021, to David Newman (San Diego) and Gregory Paul Stewart (San Mateo) for GoPro, Inc. The entry had previously said in its own text that the title and filing date were unconfirmed and had an estimated `y:2019`; corrected to 2020, `exp` 2039 → 2040, and `a` now names the two inventors. **One caveat left standing rather than smoothed over:** a twelve-month grant is fast enough to suggest a continuation, and the related-application data was not legible in the extracted text, so the 2040 expiry is an upper bound — if an earlier parent exists in the chain the real term ends sooner, and that is said in the entry. **US 12,016,421 (Forcite smart-helmet electronics) gains a figure but stays `"m"`**, which is the honest outcome: that PDF is a pure image scan with no text layer, so the report confirmed nothing about the entry and the tier cannot move on the strength of a drawing alone. Its Figures 1(a)/1(b) were rotated upright from the landscape sheet before use. Verified 159 → 160, medium 61 → 60; entries with a figure 42 → 43.
- **A second, deliberately clean test of the figure workflow — two Trek utility patents chosen specifically because they carry none of the earlier edge cases (no pre-1970 inversion, no image-only scan, no foreign format) — cleared both off the verification backlog and caught a real filing-date error.** US 6,203,042 (Trek rearward-axle-path suspension) moves `"m"` → `"v"`: the front page confirms application 09/246,439, filed February 8 1999 by Weston M. Wilcox (Sun Prairie, WI), assigned to Trek Bicycle Corporation, granted March 20 2001 — every field the atlas already had was right, including `exp:2019`, correctly computed from the non-provisional filing rather than the earlier provisional (a provisional doesn't start the 20-year clock, and the front page shows one filed a year earlier, Feb 20 1998). Gains its first figure, Fig. 2's rear-triangle close-up of the actual linkage rather than the whole-bike overview an earlier candidate offered.

  **US 8,894,083 (Trek DRCV shock) moves `"m"` → `"v"` and had a real error corrected**: the front page shows this patent is a *continuation* of application 12/704,292, filed February 11 2010 (itself granted as US 8,511,448, not separately catalogued here) — the actual priority date the 20-year term runs from. The atlas had `y:2011`/`exp:2031`, an estimate off by a year on both; corrected to `y:2010`/`exp:2030`, following the same continuation rule already established for the WickWerks/RampWerks entries. `a` now names the three actual inventors (Jose L. Gonzales, Gregory Paul Buhl, James Colegrove). **One attribution nuance worth stating plainly**: the entry's `who[]` keeps `"Fox"` and the `w` text still calls DRCV a Trek/Fox collaboration — accurate per outside reporting (Fox engineer Dennis Wooten is credited with conceiving the plunger valve, and Fox manufactured the production shocks) — but this specific patent document's own inventorship and assignee data name only Trek. Both things are true at once and the entry now says so, rather than letting "verified" quietly imply the primary source itself confirms the Fox connection.

  Verified 160 → 162, medium 60 → 58; entries with a figure 43 → 45.

- **Corrected a misattributed patent: US 11,142,133 belongs to Lolo, not 1UP USA.** The user supplied `pictures/US11142133B1.png` and flagged, from looking at the image, that the patent's subject matter and figure are Lolo's adjustable-hook design, not 1UP's — confirmed against Lolo's own official patent-info page (`loloracks.com/pages/patent`), which lists 11,142,133 ("Bike Racks Including Adjustable Hooks") as its own, and against Joseph Oshman's LinkedIn profile as Lolo Racks' owner. Moved the number from the 1UP USA independent-rack-patents entry into the Lolo bike rack portfolio entry, which now carries `nums: ["11518317","11590555","11142133"]` — three confirmed patents — and credits founder Joseph Oshman in `a`. The 1UP entry, now down to a single patent (US 10,603,968), was re-dated from the previous combined `y:2018/g:2021/exp:2039` (which had been computed across both patents together) to that patent's own record: filed Nov. 30, 2017, granted 2020 (inferred from its position in the March 2020 issue-number sequence, one step short of a directly confirmed grant date — a future session with working patent-database access should pin the exact date), `exp:2037`. Both entries' `s`/`w` text now cross-reference the correction so a reader following either patent number understands why it moved. `conf:"v"` on both, unchanged. No registry change (Lolo and 1UP USA were both already in `BRANDS`; Joseph Oshman named in `a` prose only, not added to `INVENTORS`, since this is his only entry so far — matching the atlas's existing "recurring figure only" rule for that registry). Counts unaffected: total entries, `who[]` tags, and confidence tiers all unchanged — this was a reassignment within existing entries, not a new one. The other five new pictures the user added this same pass (`US10363988B2`, `US10733795B2`, `US11091218B2`, `US11293537B2`, `US20240229892A1`) are in `pictures/` but not yet wired into any entry's `img`/`imgAlt` — a follow-up task, not done here.

- **Added US 10,549,813 to the Trust Performance linkage fork entry, and caught a mismatched file along the way.** The user pointed at `pictures/IMG_9859.jpeg` as the drawing to rename to match this number — but the sheet's own printed header reads "US 11,345,432 B2, Sheet 3 of 13," not 10,549,813, and this atlas already has a separate entry for that patent (Linkage fork with dual-taper arm). Flagged the mismatch before renaming anything; the user confirmed both patents are real and both belong to Weagle's Trust fork family, just two different, already-distinct entries in this atlas. Resolution: `num` on the Trust Performance entry (previously `null`, `conf:"m"`) is now `"10549813"` — cross-confirmed by search-snippet text quoting the patent's own claim language ("a trailing link, multi-link suspension assembly... mechanical trail distance increasing as the suspension compresses"), assignee (Trvstper, Inc.) and inventor (David Weagle), though the exact filing/grant day couldn't be read directly (this session's patent-site access is blocked per the standing network constraint) — `g` moved 2019 → 2020 based on this patent's position in the USPTO issue-number sequence, flagged in the entry's own `w` text as inferred rather than confirmed, so tier stays `"m"` rather than being upgraded on partial evidence. `IMG_9859.jpeg` was renamed to `US11345432B2_b.jpeg` (matching the existing `_b` suffix convention for a second figure on one entry, e.g. Split Pivot) and added as a second image on the dual-taper-arm entry, which converted from a single `img`/`imgAlt` to an `imgs` array holding both: the existing Fig. 6 head-tube-junction cross-section, and this new Fig. 2A, a full side-view of the fork's trailing-link geometry with the instant-center/trail construction — actually read before writing its bilingual alt text, not assumed. No entry moved tier, no count changed (total, `conf` split, and `who[]` tags all unaffected); this was a number addition to one entry plus a same-family figure correctly attributed to a different, already-verified one.

- **Folded the two Trust Performance/Trvstper entries into one portfolio card, per direct request.** "Trust Performance linkage fork (US 10,549,813)" and "Linkage fork with dual-taper arm (US 11,345,432)" are now a single entry, **"Trust Performance linkage fork patent portfolio"** — `nums:["10549813","11345432"]`, `a:"Trust / Trvstper, Inc. (Dave Weagle co-design) → Specialized"`, both images (Fig. 6 and Fig. 2A of US 11,345,432) carried over on the merged `imgs` array. `b` set to `["acquired"]` (dropping the older entry's `licensor` tag, which the surviving prose never actually supported) since the load-bearing fact requested for this pass is that **Specialized bought the patent portfolio outright** — stated explicitly in both languages now, not left implicit in an "acquired" badge alone. `conf` stays `"m"`: one of the two source patents was `"v"`, the other `"m"`, and per this atlas's own standing rule for a portfolio entry (see the confidence-tier audit above), the tier records that the card stands for a family rather than one fully-reverified number, so it takes the more conservative of the two rather than the average. `exp` set to 2038 (the later of the two patents' own expiries) as the portfolio's real protection horizon. **The entry's own text now says plainly that these two are very likely not the whole family** — Trvstper, Inc. filed a broader run of suspension-assembly patents and applications around the same period (a `USD861542S1`/`D880,372`/`20190039681`/`20200094912`/`20200094909`/`20200094911` cluster turned up by this pass's searches, none independently confirmed enough to add as their own numbers yet) — flagged as a known-incomplete portfolio rather than implying these two are the full IP picture, so a future pass adding a third or fourth Trvstper number extends this same card rather than needing a new one. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (281→280 total, 132→131 active, 173→172 verified) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings (three `<meta>` descriptions, EN/FR `shareText`, EN/FR `mtBannerText`).

- **Wired up three more figures already sitting in `pictures/` but not referenced by any card**, flagged directly by the user across three entries. **SUNringlé wheel & hub patents (US 7,334,846 + 11,090,977)** gains an `imgs` array reusing two drawing sheets already verified and captioned elsewhere in this atlas for these exact same patent numbers: `US7334846B2.png` (the Bead Socket Technology rim-profile comparison, same sheet as the Stan's NoTubes entry this patent originates from) and `US11090977B2.png` (the hub exploded assembly, same sheet as the sibling Reynolds entry) — both genuinely apply here since the entry's own text already establishes Hayes lists both numbers for SUNringlé, so this is figure reuse across a real shared-IP relationship, not a mismatch. **MRP suspension portfolio (Ribbon, Raven, Bartlett, Baxter)** gains `US11091218B2.png` as its `img` — a file the user had uploaded and whose name already matches this entry's own headline `num` (11091218); opened and read before writing `imgAlt` rather than assumed correct from the filename alone, and it checks out: two damper cross-sections (a single-cartridge coil/shim-stack damper on the left, a twin-bore mirrored-spring damper on the right) consistent with an internal-damper-architecture patent covering multiple fork models, matching what the entry's own `s` text already describes. **Lolo bike rack portfolio** gains a second image: `US11142133B1.png` (the adjustable-hook wall/mast rack, the same drawing sheet already used to catch and fix the US 11,142,133 misattribution to 1UP USA earlier in this changelog) added alongside the existing `US11518317B2.png` truck-bed-rail figure, both now in an `imgs` array. No entry's `num`/`nums`/`conf`/count changed by this pass — three cards moved from no-figure to figured, entries-with-a-figure count moves accordingly, everything else about each entry (tier, text, badges) is untouched.

- **Added a new entry: the Intend Stiffmaster headset (DE 20 2017 003 717 U1)**, placed next to the Chris King GripLock and 1990 Aheadset entries — a third headset-to-steerer clamping patent in the same cluster. Requested directly, with two images (`DE202017003717U1.png`, an exploded assembly sheet, and `DE202017003717U1.2.png`, a cross-section) supplied by the user and read before writing bilingual `imgAlt` for both. **A real document-type distinction, flagged rather than smoothed over**: the "DE 20..." number format is a German *Gebrauchsmuster* (utility model) — unexamined, self-registered on filing, with a maximum 10-year term paid in renewal stages, not the same instrument as an examined DE or EP patent. Both the entry's own `s` and `w` text say so explicitly, the same discipline this atlas already applies to the EU RCD and pre-1916 GB-patent exceptions in CLAUDE.md, though this one still gets a normal `num` (the format is deep-linkable, unlike those two). Inventor Cornelius Kapfinger and Intend Bicycle Components (Freiburg) cross-confirmed via independent press coverage (Pinkbike, ENDURO Mountainbike Magazine) describing the exact mechanism the supplied drawings show: a sleeve pressed through the head-tube cup, both bearings and the top cap, then clamped directly to the steerer with a bolt, reported to cut cockpit flex by up to 40%. `y`/`g` both set to 2017 from the document's own publication date (2017.08.31); exact filing day and whether renewal fees have kept the registration alive past its first stage weren't independently confirmed (patent-database access is blocked this session, per the standing constraint) — `conf:"v"` still applies since the primary document itself, supplied directly, is about as solid as sourcing gets in this atlas, but the day-level and maintenance-status caveats are stated in the entry's own text rather than implied away. Registered `Intend` in `BRANDS`; Kapfinger credited in `a` prose only, not added to `INVENTORS` (his only entry so far, matching the standing "recurring figure only" rule).

- **Added US 10,733,795's own figure**, per direct request — `US10733795B2.png` was already in `pictures/` but unreferenced. Fig. 2, a geometric rim-profile construction (height and width dimensions bounding a curve constrained by two radii through control points), matches this entry's own `s` text describing the patented software's parametrized-curve search — read before writing `imgAlt` rather than assumed from context.

The entry's text now also names two likely sibling design patents from the same Feb. 8, 2018 filing date — D880,371 and D880,372 — surfaced by the same searches but not independently confirmed enough to add as their own numbers this pass, left as a named lead for a future one rather than silently dropped. `conf` stays `"m"` (unchanged — a portfolio entry, per this atlas's standing rule for that shape) and `exp` stays 2038 (the design patent's own term, grant+15=2034, doesn't extend the portfolio's existing later expiry). - **Added a new entry: Dave Weagle's dual-chain high-pivot drivetrain, EP 4,059,824 A2 ("Sequential adjacent drive assembly")**, requested directly with a link to Pinkbike's coverage. Placed in the `drive` category next to the other recent pending-application entries (Shimano's direct-mount and MGU filings). Two chains in series: crank to a free-spinning driven sprocket near the pivot, which is fixed to a co-axial second sprocket driving a second chain down to the cassette — splitting the drivetrain to free up chainstay-yoke clearance, which the filing (per Pinkbike's reporting) says eats up close to 70% of a new frame's development time. `conf:"m"`, not `"v"`: Pinkbike read the filing directly and this atlas's own patent-database access is blocked, so the EP number itself — while consistent with everything independently found about this invention (inventor, mechanism, 2022 filing year, the "A2" search-report-publication status meaning not yet granted) — wasn't independently re-confirmed against the primary document this pass; said so plainly in the entry's own text rather than upgrading on partial evidence. `st:"pending"`, `g`/`exp` both `null`, matching the "A2" (unexamined publication) status. `who:["Dave Weagle"]`, no `INVENTORS` change needed (already registered). Counts synced: README At-a-glance (281→282 total, 43→44 pending, 55→56 medium) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings.

- **Added a new entry: Dave Weagle's DELTA System (WO 2010/033174), used on Evil Bikes**, requested directly with a link to Evil's own DELTA explainer and the WIPO publication number. Evil's own explainer page was blocked to this session (`evil-bikes.com` is on the standing egress-block list), but `WebFetch` on the direct `patentimages.storage.googleapis.com` PDF link the user supplied succeeded — the raw PDF came back as binary rather than extracted text, so it was saved locally and read as an actual document with the `Read` tool (needed `poppler-utils`, not previously installed in this session's container, added via `apt-get`). That's a primary-source read, not a search-snippet cross-confirmation: the WO 2010/033174 A1 front page names Evil Bikes LLC (Seattle, WA) as applicant and David Weagle as inventor, an international filing date of Sept. 15, 2009 claiming priority from a Sept. 16, 2008 US provisional, and an abstract describing exactly the low-center-of-mass, tunable-leverage linkage (shock link, wheel link, rate link moving the shock pivot down and rearward under compression) Evil markets as DELTA. Placed in `susp` right after the Split Pivot v. Trek litigation entry, keeping the existing Split Pivot → ABP "very next entry" cross-reference intact rather than breaking it up. **What wasn't confirmed, stated plainly in the entry**: a WO/PCT publication is not itself a granted right — whether this application proceeded to a granted national-phase patent (US or otherwise), and under what number, wasn't independently verified this pass (this session's patent-database access beyond the one supplied PDF link is blocked), so `g`, `exp` are `null` and `st` is `"unknown"` rather than presumed active — the same honest-gap pattern already used for the Turntable/Sotto entries in this same category. `conf:"m"`: the filing itself is primary-source-confirmed, but the compounded uncertainty about outcome keeps this out of `"v"`, matching the Turntable entry's precedent for the same shape of gap. The entry's `w` text also carries a secondhand detail worth flagging as such — reporting that Kevin Walsh bought the DELTA design from Weagle outright in 2008 alongside the company — sourced to search-result summaries of the story, not the patent document itself, and phrased with "reporting has it" rather than stated as settled fact. Registered `Evil` in `BRANDS`; Weagle already in `INVENTORS`. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (282→283 total, 155→156 brands, 56→57 medium) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings.

- **A live re-run of the figure workflow exposed one more real gap, fixed the same way as the last two.** Chose two clean modern US utility patents from the verification backlog: US 6,203,042 (Trek rearward-axle-path suspension) and US 8,894,083 (Trek DRCV shock). US 8,894,083 fetched cleanly on the first try. US 6,203,042 404'd — the kind code it was run with (a bare `A`, the only guess available without knowing the real one) doesn't exist; the actual patent is `US6203042B1`. Since every successful run so far has gone through the Google Patents fallback rather than the USPTO endpoint (which has 403'd on every GitHub-runner attempt to date), the exact kind code turns out to be load-bearing in a way secondhand sourcing usually can't supply. **Fixed at the source rather than by re-running with a corrected guess**: on a 404 specifically (not a 403, which means blocked and would fail identically under any kind code) `download_pdf()` now retries the same digits under `B1, B2, A1, A2, B, A` before giving up, and reports which one actually worked. Pinned with two mocked tests in `tools/test_fetch_patent_figure.py` that replay this exact sequence — one confirming the 404 recovery, one confirming a 403 does *not* trigger pointless guessing — since neither can be exercised against the real network from most sandboxes.

- **A production run of the figure workflow, three already-`"v"` patents chosen purely to add art rather than to clear the verification backlog: US 5,509,679 (Horst Link rear suspension), US 6,257,676 (UST tubeless system), and US 5,095,770 (Aheadset threadless headset).** All three fetched and parsed cleanly on the first try — no kind-code guessing needed since each front page's own text confirmed the real one (`A` for the two pre-2001 patents, `B1` for UST), and each front page's title/inventor/number matched the entry exactly, so no `conf` change was warranted or made. Each gained its first figure: Horst Link's Fig. 1 (the full linkage in context on the bike), UST's Figs. 1–2 together (the assembled wheel plus the rim cross-section that's the actual invention), and Aheadset's Fig. 1 (the fork/steerer/stem assembly). As with the last run, Actions was still blocked from opening its own PR by the same repository setting noted above — the candidates branch pushed fine and the PR was opened by hand from it. Entries with a figure 45 → 48; verified/medium/draft counts unchanged since none of the three needed a tier change.

- **A seven-patent figure run — four picked by request (US 8,662,517 GripLock headset, US D845,838 Knock Block stem, US 4,580,835 Hite-Rite, US 7,334,846 Bead Socket Technology) plus three more chosen the same way as the last run, purely to add art to already-`"v"` entries: US 6,206,397 (counter-rotating links / VPP), US 9,182,027 (X-Sync narrow-wide chainring), and US 8,308,179 (SWAT in-frame storage).** The original seven-number dispatch fetched cleanly on the first try for all of them; SWAT needed a second, wider run (`--candidates 9` instead of the default 4) because its actual storage-bladder cutaway (Fig. 7) sits past the default candidate window on an eight-drawing-sheet patent — the first four sheets are all a generic exploded fork/headset/stem assembly that doesn't show the invention at all. One planned pick, US 7,717,212 (Split Pivot), turned out to already carry a figure via the older `imgs:[]` array pattern that this pass's own selection script didn't check for (it only tested the singular `img` field) — the fetch briefly overwrote `pictures/US7717212B2.png` before the mismatch was caught from `git status`; the original file was restored from git and VPP (US 6,206,397) substituted in its place, an equally well-known counter-rotating-link suspension patent with no figure yet.

  Reading each front page against the entry it was meant to verify caught three real discrepancies, all fixed at the primary source rather than left standing: **US D845,838 (Knock Block)** was stated as filed July 18, 2016 / granted April 9, 2019; the patent's own front page (and an independent Google Patents cross-check) gives filed March 28, 2017 / granted April 16, 2019 — corrected, `y` moved 2016 → 2017 (grant year and `exp` were already right, design-patent term running 15 years from grant, not from filing). **US 7,334,846 (Bead Socket)** was stated as filed in 2003; the front page shows Appl. No. 10/959,743 filed directly October 6, 2004 with no continuation data on the front page — corrected, `y` 2003 → 2004 and `exp` 2023 → 2024 to match. **US 9,182,027 (X-Sync)** was stated as filed in 2012; its PDF has no text layer at all (a pure image scan, unlike every other patent in this run), so the correction came from two independent search-snippet sources instead of a direct read — Ser. No. 13/311,735, filed December 6, 2011 — `y` 2012 → 2011 and `exp` 2032 → 2031, flagged in the entry for a future session with a searchable copy to double-check. **US 6,206,397 (VPP)** turned up a genuinely harder case: its own front page states it as a continuation-in-part of the January 25, 1995 application that became US 5,553,881 (the original Outland VPP patent, already in this atlas) — `y` corrected 1999 → 1998 to match this application's own stated filing date, but whether `exp` should actually run from the 1995 parent's filing (per the continuation rule elsewhere in this doc) rather than from 1998 is a CIP patent-term question this pass didn't have the confidence to resolve, so `exp` was left at its previous value and the open question is stated directly in the entry rather than guessed at.

  Actions was again blocked from opening its own PR by the same repository setting noted in every run above — each candidates branch pushed fine and the PRs were opened by hand. Entries with a figure 48 → 55; verified 162 (unchanged, no tier changed — every correction here was a date/term fix within an already-`"v"` entry, not a tier promotion).

- **A user-supplied patent number turned out to name a different invention than the article it was pasted alongside — worth recording because it's exactly the kind of mismatch the sourcing discipline exists to catch before it becomes a fabricated entry.** Asked to add `US20220402572A1`, cited next to two articles: one about a Lauf freehub with flexible leaf-spring pawls, one about a Lauf leaf-spring rear suspension for gravel bikes. Fetching the actual PDF (egress to Google Patents is blocked from this sandbox, so this went through the same GitHub Actions figure-fetch tool used for every other verification in this doc) showed the number is the **rear suspension** application, not the freehub — 'A rear wheel suspension system for a bike,' filed by Lauf Forks hf. (Reykjavik), inventors Benedikt Skulason and Bergur Benediktsson. No new entry was created: this exact invention was already covered, bundled with the Lauf fork and the freehub, in the existing "Lauf pivotless leaf-spring fork & rear suspension" entry (`conf:"m"`, `num:null` for lack of a confirmed number). That entry now carries the verified number, its first figure (Fig. 1, the leaf-spring rear triangle), and a corrected EP priority reference — the front page shows PCT/EP2020/074808 filed September 4, 2020, priority to EP 19209794.7 filed November 18, 2019, entering the US national phase May 17, 2022 and publishing December 22, 2022 — replacing an unverifiable 'EP application 22173EP01' reference that didn't match standard EP numbering. `conf` deliberately stays `"m"` for the entry as a whole rather than moving to `"v"`: the fork itself (the entry's main subject, a decade patent-pending) and the freehub still have no confirmed number between them, and upgrading the whole entry on the strength of one now-verified sub-claim would overstate how much of it was actually checked. Because the verified number is a US filing, `j:"IS"` — previously carrying the entry's only jurisdiction tag — was dropped per the schema's own rule to omit `j` for US filings; it was the only entry using Iceland, so the "At a glance" jurisdiction count and list both move 16 → 15. The freehub patent itself remains a genuine gap: contemporary coverage describes it as filed but, as of its own reporting, not yet published to any public database, so there is still no number to verify it against — a real entry for it stays out of scope until one surfaces.

- **A second seven-patent figure run, again picked freely across categories to add art to already-`"v"` entries: US 6,843,494 (Rocky Mountain's foundational rear-suspension linkage), US 2,160,034 (Schwinn Knee-Action Spring Fork), US 7,712,566 (Schlumpf → SRAM HammerSchmidt), US 8,757,341 (Star Ratchet EXP), US D204,121 (Schwinn Sting-Ray banana seat), US 5,833,074 (QuikRack), and US 10,021,931 (helmet-chin camera mount, the `tech` category's first-ever figure).** The candidate query surfaced something the original seventh pick couldn't use: US 7,665,929 (a BMX/MTB starting gate) turned out to have **two separate entries for the same number** — one full and already pictured (`comp`, Prostuff LLC, filed 2007/granted 2010, exp 2027) and one thinner duplicate (`frame`, no assignee, filed 2006/granted 2010, exp 2026) that had drifted to a conflicting filing year and expiry without ever being caught. Nothing referenced the thinner one (no `FIGHTS` card, no shared `who[]` registrant), so it was removed outright rather than illustrated a second time, and US 10,021,931 substituted in as the seventh pick.

  Reading each front page against its entry caught three more real discrepancies, same discipline as the run before this one. **US 7,712,566** was catalogued as Florian Schlumpf's own 1988-filed/1992-granted Swiss patent (`j:"CH"`); the actual document is SRAM's own later "Bicycle transmission" continuation, invented by Brian Jordan, Kevin Wesling and Christopher Shipman — filed August 8, 2008 as a continuation of an April 11, 2007 application, granted May 11, 2010. `y` 1988 → 2008, `g` 1992 → 2010, `j:"CH"` dropped (it's a plain US number, not a Swiss one), and `exp` corrected 2030 → 2027 per the continuation-term rule: term runs from the 2007 parent filing, not this document's own 2008 filing. The Schlumpf-to-SRAM acquisition narrative in the entry's prose and `a` field is carried forward as prior research, not re-verified this pass. **US 8,757,341 (Star Ratchet EXP)** was stated as filed 2012/granted 2013; the front page — independently cross-checked, since its two-column layout extracted too scrambled to trust on its own — gives filed August 2, 2011/granted June 24, 2014. `y` 2012 → 2011, `g` 2013 → 2014; `exp` stays 2032, which turns out to already be right on its own terms, since the grant carries a 296-day patent term adjustment past the flat 20-year date — now stated explicitly in the entry rather than landing on the correct number by coincidence. **US 5,833,074 (QuikRack)** carried `exp:2016`; filed April 6, 1995 — three months before the June 8, 1995 GATT cutoff — falls under the pre-GATT rule (grant + 17, not filing + 20), giving 2015, not 2016, corrected in both the field and the prose sentence that had referenced the wrong year directly. Also checked and left alone: the helmet-chin-mount entry (US 10,021,931) carries `st:"expired"` against an `exp:2035` nine years out, which read like the same kind of bug on sight — but its own text already explains this correctly (a documented USPTO maintenance-fee lapse, not a term expiration), so it was left as-is rather than "fixed" into a wrong state.

  Actions was blocked from opening its own PR again, the same recurring repository-permission gap as every run before it; the candidate branch pushed fine and the PR was opened by hand. Entries with a figure 50 → 57; total entries 274 → 273 (the removed duplicate); active 133 → 132; verified 162 → 161; non-US jurisdictions 15 → 14 (CH's only remaining entry was the corrected Schlumpf one, above).

- **Two requested figures, one of which turned into a real primary-source correction rather than a plain art addition.** US 9,234,553 (Shimano Ice Tech disc rotor) fetched cleanly — front page confirmed filed December 29, 2011 / granted January 12, 2016, an exact match to what the entry already stated, so no correction was needed; gained Fig. 1 (the rotor mounted in a caliper/fork assembly, with the exposed aluminum cooling fin visible in each spider arm). The "Star Ratchet freehub (original)" entry was a harder case: it carried `num:null`, `conf:"m"`, and a secondhand `y:1994/g:1995/exp:2020` that had never been checked against an actual document. Cross-confirming inventor and assignee across several independent search queries before spending a fetch run on a guess turned up **US 5,676,227**, "Free-wheel hub for bicycles" — inventor Wilfried Hügi, assigned to DT-Bike Technology AG of Biel, Switzerland (the entity behind the DT Hügi hub line) — a strong enough match (name, assignee, era, and a clutch mechanism description matching the Star Ratchet's face-toothed spring-separated rings) to spend the fetch run on. The primary source's own front page reads filed November 30, 1995 / granted October 14, 1997, with a December 2, 1994 German utility-model priority — likely the real origin of the "introduced in 1994" story, but not the US filing date that sets the US term. Since November 30, 1995 falls after the June 8, 1995 GATT cutoff, the term is filing + 20 = **2015**, not the 2020 this entry previously (and unverifiably) stated. Corrected `y` 1994 → 1995, `g` 1995 → 1997, `exp` 2020 → 2015, `num` null → `"5676227"`, `conf` `"m"` → `"v"`; gained Fig. 1 (the full hub cross-section along the axle). The entry's own text now says plainly that the old "January 2020" figure didn't match the document and may have referred to a different, later DT Swiss patent this pass didn't independently locate, rather than quietly swapping one unverified date for another. Entries with a figure 57 → 59; verified 161 → 162; medium 58 → 57; total entries unchanged at 273, so the six hardcoded `index.html` count strings needed no edit.

- **Fixed a stale internal cross-reference the user caught directly: the atlas's "un-patent" thread (Boost → Triple Triangle → Forward Geometry, each numbered "second"/"third" alongside the last) had already drifted once — ISIS Drive's own entry calls itself "the fourth open-standard node," a number Forward Geometry's "third" claim didn't account for — and was missing a fifth member entirely: the Matyas fat-bike hub-spacing entry (170/177x12, 190/197x12), which is exactly the same shape of case (`num:null`, "no patent" in its own title, `b:["priorart","givenaway"]`) but had never been cross-referenced into the family.** Rather than patch the ordinal to "fifth" and leave the same fragility for the next addition, dropped the numbered-position framing from the Forward Geometry and ISIS Drive entries' `w:` text in favor of naming the current full set directly, and added a matching cross-reference sentence to the Matyas entry so all three (plus Boost and Triple Triangle, unchanged — their own text doesn't claim a closed list) now point at the same five-member family. No field values changed, so all counts are unchanged; this was purely a prose-consistency fix.

- **Added the Cannondale HeadShok/Lefty entry's first figure.** The entry already carried both patent numbers (US 5,308,099 and US 5,509,674, the divisional of the same original 1990 application) at `conf:"v"`; fetching both PDFs confirmed the existing dates exactly — 5,308,099 filed Sept. 26, 1990 / granted May 3, 1994; 5,509,674 filed as a division May 2, 1994 / granted Apr. 23, 1996, assigned to Cannondale Corporation — so no correction was needed. Used Figs. 2–3 of US 5,308,099 (the telescoping steerer's coil-spring stack in cross-section) rather than the plain whole-bike Fig. 1, since the head tube in Fig. 1 is drawn as an unremarkable rectangle and doesn't actually show the suspension mechanism the entry is about. One thing noted but not acted on: 5,308,099's own front page shows no assignee line at all (an individual inventor filing, Michael R. S. Browning), while the divisional 5,509,674 explicitly assigns to Cannondale — consistent with the entry's existing "same assignee" text if rights were assigned between the two filings, but that assignment itself wasn't independently confirmed this pass. Entries with a figure 59 → 60.

- **Added a second figure to the same Cannondale HeadShok/Lefty entry on request, moving it from the singular `img`/`imgAlt` fields to the `imgs[]` array** — same pattern as Becker, DW-Link and Split Pivot elsewhere in this doc. No new fetch was needed — the candidate was still in scratch from the same GitHub Actions run. No field/date changes, no count changes beyond the image itself.

- **Added three more Cannondale patents the user queued in a prior session, all fetched and confirmed via the GitHub Actions figure tool, all `conf:"v"`.** **US 4,513,985** ("Bicycle frame seat stays," filed Jan. 6 1983 / granted Apr. 30 1985, David R. Graham) — the Optimum Oval Seatstays patent, claiming an oval seatstay cross-section oriented for more lateral than vertical stiffness; pre-GATT term (grant+17) puts `exp` at 2002, not the post-GATT filing+20 an entry this old could easily be miscalculated with. Category `frame`, figure from Sheet 1 (Figs. 1–2, the oval profile at the seat cluster and dropout). **US 6,497,427** ("Bicycle frame," PCT-filed June 9 2000, US national stage May 2001, granted Dec. 24 2002, Christopher P. D'Alusio & Christopher A. Peck) — claims a minimum ~2-inch seat-stay gap for rear-disc-brake tire/mud clearance, bounded by a specific stiffness range rather than just a shape; term runs from the PCT filing date, `exp` 2020, already expired. Category `frame`, figure is Fig. 2 (the gap-distance dimension the claim is actually about, not the whole-frame perspective view). **US 4,271,996** ("Pannier for bicycle rear carrier racks," filed Mar. 30 1979 / granted June 9 1981, Joseph S. Montgomery) — a touring pannier bag stiffened by an internal rib frame that also extends the carrier rack's own load-bearing top surface; pre-GATT `exp` 1998. Placed in `comp`, not `transport` — this atlas's `transport` category is specifically for racks that carry *bikes* on a vehicle (QuikRack, Kuat, Thule, etc.), and a bike-mounted rack that carries cargo is the opposite direction, so `comp` (general accessories) is the better fit. The user's supplied "early 1981" description for this one matches the grant date; the actual filing was 1979, noted in the entry's own text rather than silently corrected. Cannondale was already registered in `BRANDS`; no new registrations needed. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (273→276 total, 90→93 expired, 162→165 verified) and all eight hardcoded `index.html` count strings (three meta tags, the CSS-comment entry-count note, EN/FR `shareText`, EN/FR `mtBannerText`).

- **A second queued item, Push Industries' Elevensix shock, was investigated but NOT added — the three patent numbers supplied for it (US 10,036,442 B2, US 9,897,160 B2, US 10,816,055 B2, sourced from a Gemini-generated search result) do not belong to Push Industries at all.** Running all three through the same GitHub Actions figure-fetch tool and reading each front page directly shows: US 10,036,442 B2 is AAC Technologies Pte. Ltd. (Singapore)'s "Elastic member and vibrating motor using same" (a phone speaker-motor part); US 9,897,160 B2 is Bridgestone Corporation (Japan)'s "Vibration isolator" (an automotive/industrial engine-mount patent); US 10,816,055 B2 is Tenneco Automotive Operating Company Inc.'s "Damper bumper cap with labyrinth air passageway" (an automotive shock-absorber seal component). All three are real, resolvable patents in the same general damping/vibration technology space as a bicycle shock — which is almost certainly why an AI-generated list produced them — but none are Push Industries', none are bicycle patents, and none are Elevensix. Independent `WebSearch` passes (both before and after the fetch) could not locate Push's actual Dual Overhead Valve patent number either; the closest primary-source lead found was Justia's real assignee listing for "Push Industries Incorporated," which shows a *different* product (a fork-assembly application, publication 20240308619, filed March 2024, inventor Matt White — not Darren W. Murphy as the supplied summary stated). Per this repo's sourcing discipline, none of the three supplied numbers were used. Push Industries (Loveland, CO) remains unregistered in `BRANDS`/`BRAND_HQ` and Elevensix remains unadded — this needs either a correct patent number from a future search pass or a `conf:"m"`/`num:null` entry citing the well-documented "patented Dual Overhead Valve" marketing claim without a specific number, the same treatment this atlas already gives Foxfolk's Dirtskirt. Flagged back to the user rather than guessed around.

GRIP2 gained Fig. 2, an isometric of the top-cap assembly showing the concentric high/low-speed compression dials; VelociRAX gained Fig. 1, the whole-rack isometric with the stacked C-hoop cradles. **US 9,643,683** (Rocky Mountain's Dyname electric propulsion system) — front page confirmed the assignee is Propulsion PowerCycle Inc., exactly matching the entry's own existing "licensed exclusively to Rocky Mountain" framing rather than contradicting it; gained the composite Figs. 1A–1C sheet (motor unit on the complete bike, a detail view, and the internal transmission schematic).

  One patent could not be added this pass: **US 12,491,966** (Porsche's wheel motor-brake unit) failed at the fetch step itself — the USPTO print endpoint returned 403 even from the GitHub Actions runner (open egress), and the Google Patents page it fell back to didn't expose a `patentimages` PDF link, most likely because the patent is too recently granted to be fully indexed there yet. Rather than leave a gap in the batch, US 9,643,683 (Dyname) was substituted in as the seventh pick; Porsche's entry remains unillustrated and unblocked for a future pass once the PDF becomes reachable by one of the usual routes.

- **Added both figures for the DE 10 2022 204 483 A1 entry above, uploaded by the user directly to `main` via GitHub's web UI** (`pictures/DE102022204483A1.png`, `pictures/DE102022204483A1.1.png`) since the atlas's own fetch tooling only handles US-numbered PDFs and had no route to a German publication on its own. Moved the entry from no figure to a two-item `imgs[]` array, matching the Becker/DW-Link/Cannondale HeadShok pattern used elsewhere for multi-figure entries. **Fig. 1** is the system diagram: the bicycle's handlebar-mounted operating unit (4, wireless module 7a) and frame-mounted drive/control unit (3, 11), each communicating wirelessly with a smartphone (5, app 7b) and a remote server (10) inside a cloud (9) — the mode-selection UI and its connectivity, matching the abstract directly. **Fig. 3** is a pair of assistance-vs-speed graphs, each rising to a peak then falling to zero at a marked maximum speed (V max), with a reference speed (V AP) called out on the descending slope; the two curves differ in shape, consistent with (though not independently confirmed as) distinct assistance profiles for different selectable support modes. Updated the entry's `w:` text to drop the now-stale "no figure sourced" line. `conf` stays `"v"`, no other fields changed. Since main had a genuine, un-mergeable divergent commit (the direct picture upload) by the time this branch was ready to go live, brought it in with a merge commit rather than a fast-forward before pushing. Counts synced: "entries with a figure" moves 100 → 101 (`imgs[]` 7 → 8); no total/status/tier counts affected, so no README At-a-glance or `index.html` hardcoded-string changes needed beyond this bullet.

- **Upgraded the "TTX twin-tube damper" entry from a `conf:"m"` placeholder (`num:null`) to a verified, dual-patent portfolio, on two numbers the user found and two more he supplied pictures for that turned out to belong elsewhere entirely.** The user surfaced **EP 2,242,939 B1** ("Shock absorber with dual piston") and **USD 752,482 S** ("Rear shock absorber for a bicycle") as candidates and uploaded six pictures directly to `main` via GitHub's web UI. Verifying all six against primary sources (the GitHub Actions figure tool for the five US-parseable numbers, cross-confirmed `WebSearch` for the EP-only one, since this sandbox's egress to Google Patents/USPTO stayed blocked throughout) found a genuine split: **two matched the TTX story exactly**, and **four were unrelated inventions that happen to belong to two other, already-`conf:"v"` entries in this same atlas** — the same "user-supplied numbers point to a different invention" failure mode flagged for Push Elevensix and 1UP USA in earlier passes, just caught before anything wrong went in rather than after.
  Confirmed for TTX: **EP 2,242,939 B1**, Öhlins Racing AB (Upplands Väsby, Sweden), filed Dec 17, 2008 as the EP/PCT phase of a Dec 19, 2007 Swedish priority filing, granted Aug 28, 2019 — cross-confirmed via two independent `WebSearch` queries since the EP document itself can't be fetched directly or through the US-only figure tool; its identical US counterpart, **US 8,807,302 B2** (same assignee, inventors Nygren & Jarl, granted Aug 19, 2014), was fetched and read directly as the primary source for the underlying mechanism. **USD 752,482 S**, same assignee, "Rear shock absorber for a bicycle," filed Jan 22, 2015 (a division of a June 2013 filing), granted Mar 29, 2016 with a 14-year term stated on its own front page — fetched and read directly. `y` 2003→2008, `g` 2005→2019, `num` null→`"EP2242939B1"`, `nums` added (`["EP2242939B1","D752482"]`), `j` "SE"→"EP", `exp` 2025→2030 (the later-expiring of the two patents, matching the Cannondale HeadShok/DW-Link convention for multi-patent entries), `conf` "m"→"v". Both user-supplied pictures wired in as a two-item `imgs[]`: Fig. 1a/1b of the EP patent — labeled "Prior art" in the document itself, so captioned honestly as the baseline twin-tube architecture the patent improves on, not a picture of the specific claimed dual-piston refinement — and Fig. 1 of the design patent, the shock body's ornamental isometric view.
  **Misdirected, not TTX-related, and re-homed to where they actually belong:** **US 6,494,117 B1** ("Pedal/cleat assembly," Richard M. Bryne/Speedplay) is already `nums[1]` of this atlas's own "Bryne / Speedplay later pedal-interface patents" entry — gained its first figure (Fig. 1, the exploded cleat-body/spring-tongue assembly). **US 6,155,394** ("Bicycle coasting mechanism," Bill Shook/American Classic) is already the primary `num` of "Bill Shook / American Classic hub & drivetrain portfolio" — gained its first figure (Fig. 9, the ratchet ring's evenly spaced internal teeth). Both entries were already `conf:"v"`; no date/number corrections needed, this was purely a picture-wiring bonus surfaced by chasing down what the uploaded files actually were.
  **Two pictures — US 6,199,021 B1 ("Method and apparatus for measuring power output of one powering a chain driven vehicle," CC Kinetics, Inc.) and US 2015/0008059 A1 ("Drive device for bicycles," Philip Douglas & Horst Gusterhuber) — don't match any existing atlas entry.** Confirmed via the same GitHub Actions fetch (front pages read directly): the first is an early bicycle chain-tension/speed power meter, unrelated to any brand currently in this atlas; the second is a rear-swingarm drive unit shown in the patent's own figures on a wheelchair-conversion cycle and enclosed cargo trike, not a mountain bike. Neither was wired anywhere or used to start a new entry — flagged back to the user rather than guessed into place, since neither this session nor a keyword search found what either was meant to illustrate.
