"Entries with a figure" moves 101 → 104 (`img` 93→95, `imgs[]` 8→9).

- **Verified the Leatt-Brace entry on a number and two figures the user supplied, and caught a status change the correction forces.** **US 8,562,551 B2**, "Neck Brace," Christopher James Leatt (Constantia, South Africa), assigned to Xceed Holdings CC (Cape Town) — fetched and read directly via the GitHub Actions figure tool. Granted Oct. 22, 2013 as a continuation of a May 25, 2006 application (issued as US 7,993,293), itself a continuation of the original PCT filing (PCT/ZA2004/000148, filed Nov. 26, 2004), claiming priority to a Nov. 26, 2003 South African filing (ZA 2003/9174) — the year after the 2001 racetrack fatality Leatt has said motivated the design. Per the continuation-term rule, the 20-year clock runs from that 2004 PCT filing, not this document's own 2011 filing date, giving an estimated expiry of **2024 — already passed**; the patent's own front page confirms zero days of patent-term adjustment and a terminal disclaimer tying its term to the earlier-filed parent, so nothing extends past that date. `y` unchanged at 2004 (was already the right year, just previously unverified), `g` 2006→2013 (the earlier draft date likely referred to a different, still-unidentified South African patent in Leatt's 30+-patent portfolio, or was simply a guess), `num` null→`"8562551"`, `j` "ZA" dropped (schema rule: omit `j` for a plain US filing — this was the *only* atlas entry using ZA, so it drops out of the jurisdiction list entirely), `st` active→**expired**, `exp` null→2024, `conf` "m"→"v". Both user-supplied figures wired in as a two-item `imgs[]`: Fig. 1 (the brace worn under a helmet, showing how its ring catches the helmet's rear edge to limit tilt) and Fig. 2 (a hand-annotated plan view of the two-piece ring and its strap routing). Counts synced: README At-a-glance (132→131 active, 94→95 expired, 171→172 verified, 56→55 medium, 14→13 non-US jurisdictions with ZA removed from the list; total/pending/litigated unchanged) and confirmed the three `index.html` meta descriptions, `shareText`, and `mtBannerText` needed no edit (total unchanged at 280). "Entries with a figure" moves 104 → 105 (`imgs[]` 9→10).

- **Investigated the user's suspicion that the Nicolai gear-transmission portfolio entry's "Colorado Innovation" panel tag was a bug, since Nicolai Bicycles is a German company — confirmed it's not a bug.** The panel tag is computed at render time from `who[]` against the `BRAND_HQ` registry (`isColorado` in `cardHTML()`), not stored on the entry itself. This entry's `who` is `["Nicolai","Gates"]`; `"Nicolai"` has no `BRAND_HQ` mapping at all, but `"Gates"` maps to `"Colorado"` (The Gates Corporation, Denver) — already registered for other entries before this one. The tag is legitimately triggered by Gates' real presence in this portfolio (2 of the 5 patents are assigned directly to Gates, per the entry's own `s:` text), exactly matching the user's own guess. No `s`/`w` text contains a stray "Colorado" string either, so there's no secondary false-positive path to worry about. No code or data change made — flagged back to the user with the actual mechanism rather than silently leaving the question open or removing a correct tag.

- **Gave each of the four visual themes its own display font, and removed CLAUDE.md's former "two Google Fonts total" non-negotiable at the user's explicit request.** `--font-display` (the CSS variable already driving `h1`, tab labels, era/stats/fights headings) now resolves to a different family per theme instead of always falling back to Barlow Condensed: **Nunito** for WMBC (soft/rounded, matching its organic trail identity), **Bitter** for COMBA (a slab serif for its "heavy serif-feel" vintage-editorial brief), **Anton** for CAMBA (an ultra-bold condensed face for its bold-racing/urgent brief), and **IBM Plex Mono** for CAMBR (matching its own "mono-forward" technical-blueprint description). Patent titles, body copy, and buttons are untouched — they stay on the shared Barlow/Barlow Condensed pair, so only headings and structural labels shift per theme. All four are loaded from the same Google Fonts `<link>` already in `<head>`; no new external host, no build step. Six font families total now load with the page instead of two — a real, deliberate increase in page weight for an iframed/mobile-embedded tool, accepted here because the user asked for the constraint removed rather than worked around.

- **Added the missing figure to the "Crampon convex pedal platform" entry (US D615,003 S) — the atlas's first Canfield pedal design patent, which already existed as a `conf:"v"` text-only entry sitting right next to its sequel, "Crampon pedal, second design" (US D667,348), which already had one.** Fetched and cross-checked via the GitHub Actions figure tool, since this sandbox's egress to Google Patents/USPTO stayed blocked throughout. The front page confirms the entry's existing data exactly — Christopher and Lance Canfield, filed May 13, 2009, granted May 4, 2010, and a stated **14-year** design-patent term (the rule for anything filed before May 13, 2015; `exp:2024` was already computed correctly) — and caught one small existing inaccuracy along the way: the entry's `w:` text said "15 years," which the front page directly contradicts. Fixed in both languages; `conf` stays `"v"`. Gained **Fig. 1**, the pedal's perspective view showing the convex dome-up platform, tapered edges, and axle boss — the source PDF page itself was rotated 90°, so the cropped image was rotated back (matching the fix applied earlier to the "Integrated active valve assembly" figure) rather than left sideways. No `D` entries added, no counts affected beyond "entries with a figure" (105 → 106, `img` 95 → 96).

- **Registered Push Industries and added the ElevenSix's "Dual Overhead Valve" shock-controller patent, closing out a long-flagged gap.** Two prior passes couldn't find a real number: one investigated three candidates supplied from an AI search summary (US10036442B2, US9897160B2, US10816055B2) and found all three genuine but unrelated — a phone vibration motor, an automotive vibration isolator, a shock bumper cap, none Push's. This pass found it independently via `WebSearch` cross-confirmation (assignee, inventor, and filing chain agreeing across multiple queries), then fetched and read the primary source directly via the GitHub Actions figure tool to confirm before adding. **US 10,156,280 B2**, "Control valve to permit adjustability of a shock absorber" — filed Sept. 9, 2015 (claiming priority to a Sept. 9, 2014 provisional, which per this atlas's own continuation rule doesn't itself count toward the term), granted Dec. 18, 2018, inventor Darren W Murphy (Push Industries' own founder), assignee Push Industries Incorporated, Loveland, CO. The claims are an exact match for the ElevenSix's marketed mechanism: a first valve and a second valve, each independently tunable to its own damping rate, plus a flow director routing shock fluid to one or the other — two completely separate, on-the-fly-selectable damping circuits from one shock. `exp` 2035 (filing+20, post-GATT). `conf:"v"`. Registered **Push Industries** in `BRANDS` and `BRAND_HQ` (Colorado, joining Gates/MRP/Revel/Reeb/Spot) — Darren Murphy was not added to `INVENTORS`, matching this atlas's convention of not filter-tagging a one-off company founder credited in `a`. Gained **Fig. 1**, an end view of the reservoir/controller housing showing its two identical valve assemblies side by side — the flow director's twin paths that give the shock its two damping circuits. This exact file (`pictures/US10156280B2.png`) already existed in the repo's history — uploaded and later deleted (orphaned) in an earlier, abandoned attempt at this same entry — so the pre-existing, correctly-oriented crop was restored rather than replaced with a rougher one freshly fetched via the GitHub Actions tool for this pass (which produced a whole-bike-context figure instead; discarded in favor of the existing file once found). Placed in `cat:"fork"` next to the TTX twin-tube damper entry, matching this atlas's existing convention of filing rear-shock-internals patents under `fork` rather than `susp` (which is reserved for frame-linkage/kinematic patents). Counts synced: README At-a-glance (280→281 total, 131→132 active, 153→154 brands, 172→173 verified; expired/pending/litigated/inventors/jurisdictions unchanged) and all six hardcoded `index.html` count strings (3 meta tags, EN/FR `shareText`, EN/FR `mtBannerText`). "Entries with a figure" moves 106 → 107 (`img` 96 → 97).

- **Closed out the Bead Socket Technology entry's own long-standing gap: it described itself as "one of four patents in Stan's ZTR portfolio" but only ever carried the one, foundational number.** The user supplied five candidate numbers from an AI search summary — the same risky source that produced three unrelated patents for the Push Industries entry above — so each was independently verified rather than trusted: cross-confirmed via `WebSearch` (consistent assignee, inventor, and continuation chain across multiple independent trade-press and Google Patents snippets), then read directly via the GitHub Actions figure tool's front-page reports before touching the entry. All four new numbers checked out as genuine, and the chain is tighter than even the atlas's own prior text assumed — five patents, not four, in one unbroken continuation lineage: **US 8,007,053** (continuation filed Apr. 8, 2009, granted Aug. 30, 2011, rim-to-tire interface dimensions), **US 8,613,485** (continuation filed Aug. 26, 2011, granted Dec. 24, 2013, interior-shape/structural-integrity parameters), **US 9,162,524** (continuation filed Nov. 25, 2013, granted Oct. 20, 2015, interior-profile/sidewall refinements), and **US 9,649,878** (continuation filed Sep. 21, 2015, granted Apr. 7, 2017, sidewall-height/dimensional-range claims) — each explicitly citing the previous one as its parent application, all five tracing back to the original Oct. 6, 2004 filing (Appl. No. 10/959,743) that issued as US 7,334,846. Per this atlas's continuation rule, a continuation inherits its parent's filing date rather than its own, so all five share the same 2024 expiry already on record — `exp` did not change. Also surfaced a fact new to this atlas: all five list assignee **K.G. Motors, Inc.** (Horseheads, NY), Stanley Koziatek's patent-holding entity, not "Stan's NoTubes" directly — folded into the entry's `s` text rather than changed in `a`, which stays the reader-facing brand name. Added `nums:["7334846","8007053","8613485","9162524","9649878"]`, retitled to "Bead Socket Technology portfolio," and added a sourced-from-trade-press mention of the 2017 Velocity BST licensing deal to `w`. No new `D` entry, no image change (the existing US7334846B2.png rim-cross-section figure already illustrates the shared core geometry all five patents protect), `conf` stays `"v"`, and no counts moved since total/status/tier are all unaffected.

- **Added a figure for each of the four Stan's continuations above, converting the entry's single `img` to a five-item `imgs[]` array.** Fetched via the GitHub Actions figure tool at a higher candidate count (6 sheets) for the two later continuations once the first pass showed they might carry genuinely new drawings, not just the parent's reused set — they did. The family turns out to hold two drawing generations, not five independent sets: **US 8,007,053** and **US 8,613,485** file the identical Fig. 3/4 sheet (tire bead seated in the channel, plus the channel's dimensioned width/height/offset) — disclosed as shared in both captions rather than presented as if each had unique art. **US 9,162,524** and **US 9,649,878** both add a second, expanded sheet on top of the original Fig. 1–4 (also shared between those two): Fig. 5's small interior-channel step, Fig. 6's foam/elastomer insert at the rim-well base, Fig. 7's fully hatched wall-thickness cross-section, and Fig. 8's hanging load-test rig — genuinely new drawings introduced a decade into the continuation chain, not reused from 2004. Picked one distinct page per patent (alternating Fig. 1/2 → parent's existing image, Fig. 3/4 → the two mid-chain continuations, Fig. 5/6 and Fig. 7/8 → the two later ones) so the five cards read as five different pictures rather than near-duplicates, while each `alt` text says plainly which other patent(s) share that exact sheet. All four images are correctly oriented as fetched, no rotation needed. Counts: "entries with a figure" unchanged at 107 (this entry already counted as illustrated before this pass) — only the `img`/`imgs[]` split moves, 97→96 / 10→11.

- **Added figures to seven verified patents that lacked drawings.** From the set identified as illustration candidates — 28 verified entries with real numbers but no images — seven patents were successfully fetched and cross-checked via the GitHub Actions figure tool. The first five: **US 10,926,830 B2** (Yeti suspension linkage, FIG. 5 showing the multi-axis extension body geometry), **US D1,023,842** (Yeti shock extension design patent, housing and mounting interface), **US 9,404,552** (B-LABS/Fox three-way damping cartridge, FIG. 2 internal piston-valve cross-section), **US 7,163,222** (Fox fork damper architecture, FIG. 3 showing the cartridge's oil-flow paths), and **US 11,345,432** (Specialized/Weagle linkage fork, FIG. 6 showing the dual-taper arm geometry and pivot axis). Plus two additional US patents: **US 11,179,979** (Sport Components/milKit tubeless valve, FIG. 2 showing the cartridge seal and deployment mechanism) and **US 5,426,570** (McKay H. Davis water-bottle-cage light, FIG. 2 showing the LED mount assembly). Each front page was read to confirm existing entry data (assignees, filing/grant dates, inventors, titles all matched) and `conf` stays `"v"` across the board. Bilingual alt text added to each, describing the figure number shown and the key mechanism reference numerals visible. "Entries with a figure" moves 107 → 114 (`img` 96 → 103). Three candidates remain outstanding — AU 2013225624 (I-Track, Australian filing; network access limited), US 2024/0229892 (Fox tuned mass damper, published application format handling), and FR 86705 (Meyer wheel, 1869 French patent; pre-modern numbering scheme) — each presents specific challenges (international databases, non-PDF formats, or pre-1916 archives) that will require targeted approaches in subsequent passes.
- **Added a MTB/ALL range toggle to the top-nav "Patents per era" bar chart, next to the existing 2yr/1yr/5yr bracket toggle.** Requested directly. **MTB** (default) scopes the chart to 1980–present; **ALL** widens it to the full dataset, back to the 1869 entries. Chart-only, by design: the new `eraScope` state var feeds a dedicated `getChartEras()` (wrapping the existing `buildEras(step, srcD)`, given an optional source array) used only by `renderBars()`, while the main timeline’s own card grouping keeps calling the original, unscoped `getEras()` — so switching to MTB narrows the chart’s own axis without hiding a single pre-1980 card from the timeline itself, the Fights view, or the Stats tab. New `MTB_ERA_START` constant (1980), chosen as a few years after Joe Breeze’s 1977 JBX1 prototype (see below) — mountain biking’s own purpose-built era, as distinct from the 1869–1913 general-bicycle-mechanics entries that predate the sport. Bilingual button label (`eraScopeLabel`: "MTB"/"ALL" in English, "VTT"/"TOUT" in French) and tooltip (`eraScopeTip`), wrapped with the step-bracket button in a new `.scrub-toggles` flex row so both sit together in the scrubber’s top-right corner. No visual/browser check available this pass — noted per this file’s own "no committed test suite" convention.

- **Added the JBX1 "Breezer 1" — the frame, not a patent.** Requested directly, as a deliberately non-patent historical marker: hand-built by Joe Breeze in 1977, the JBX1 (later "Breezer 1") was the first bicycle frame purpose-designed for off-road riding rather than a converted cruiser or road frame, built for the Repack downhill races on Mount Tamalpais — the object that turned mountain biking from modified "klunkers" into a purpose-built category. No patent was ever filed on the frame itself; Breezer #1 is held in the Smithsonian’s National Museum of American History. `num: null`, `st:"unknown"` (no patent, so no patent-status field applies — same convention as the existing Fatback/Matyas "no patent" entry), `conf:"v"` (cross-confirmed across the Smithsonian’s own catalog record, Wikipedia, and contemporaneous trade coverage). `w` text also notes mountain biking’s fast institutional rise afterward — a UCI-sanctioned World Championship by 1990 (Durango, Colorado) and an Olympic discipline at the 1996 Atlanta Games. Placed in `cat:"frame"`, right after the 1938 Schwinn cantilever frame entry. Joe Breeze was already registered in `INVENTORS` from his separate Hite-Rite patent entry, so no registry addition was needed. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (283→284 total, 173→174 verified) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings (three meta tags, EN/FR `shareText`, EN/FR `mtBannerText`).


- **Confirmed the Chilao SS "Static Spring" pedal axle patent: US 12,391,334 B1, "Bicycle pedal," inventor Yusaku Yoshimura.** Previously a `conf:"l"` draft placeholder (`num:null, st:"pending"`). The user supplied the number directly via a Google Patents URL filtered to that inventor; independently, a separate WebSearch pass surfaced a USPTO listing for the same number under the same title with a matching mechanism description (a spring member absorbing axial impact and preventing bearing binding from wear), cross-confirming the number and subject without relying on the user’s link alone. **One AI-generated search summary this pass repeatedly misattributed this same number to a different inventor (“Chang-Hsin Wu”) and an unrelated force-sensor mechanism — not trusted, matching this repo’s own documented experience with this exact failure mode (see the RatioX note earlier in this changelog).** `st` moved `"pending"` → `"active"` (a B1 patent number is a grant, not an application) and the title dropped “(patent pending).” **`g` and `exp` are deliberately left `null`** rather than guessed: this session could not independently reopen the Google Patents record itself (blocked to this sandbox) to read the actual filing/grant dates, so nothing here states a date this pass didn’t verify. `conf` raised `"l"` → `"m"` — the number and inventor are confirmed, but the dates aren’t, which is exactly what `"m"` is for. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (132→133 active, 44→43 pending, 57→58 medium, 53→52 draft; total and verified unchanged).

- **Merged the duplicate SUNringlé/Reynolds US 11,090,977 entries into one card, and removed the erroneous co-listing of Stan’s US 7,334,846 on the SUNringlé card.** Reported directly by the user, who’d found the same number (11,090,977) rendering as two separate cards — a 2003-filed "SUNringlé wheel & hub patents" card that incorrectly bundled it with Stan’s 7,334,846 (and dated the *pair* to 2003/2008, which is right for 7,334,846 but wrong for 11,090,977, actually filed 2018/granted 2021 per the sibling Reynolds card), and a separate 2018-filed "Reynolds wheel patent" card carrying the correct dates for that same number. Since Hayes Bicycle Group owns both SUNringlé and Reynolds and the patent itself already said as much (both cards’ own `w` text called it shared IP), the fix was to merge them into a single card — `y:2018, g:2021, exp:2038` (the correct dates), `who:["SUNringlé","Reynolds","Hayes"]`, `num:"11090977"` only — rather than carry two cards for one number with disagreeing dates on one of them. The Stan’s 7,334,846 licensing fact wasn’t dropped, just relocated to where it belongs: a new sentence in the existing **Bead Socket Technology portfolio** entry’s `w` text (Stan’s own card) noting Hayes’ SUNringlé marking page also lists that number, mirroring the sentence already there about the Velocity license, with a pointer back to this merged card for the other side of the arrangement. **Known side effect: this breaks the two old `#p=11090977-1`/`#p=11090977-2` deep links** (e.g. ones already shared or embedded elsewhere) — the disambiguating `-1`/`-2` suffix only exists when two cards share a `num`, so with one card left, its link is now the bare `#p=11090977`. No fix applied for the stale links themselves (there’s no card left for `-1`/`-2` to point at, by design — that was the bug), flagged here in case either old link is live anywhere. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (284→283 total, 133→132 active, 174→173 verified; all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings updated to match) — net -1 from the merge, since 284 was reached the same session by two separate additions (JBX1, Chilao SS) that both remain.

Retitled to "Hydra high-engagement system (US 11,293,500 + 12,326,175)" and added `nums:["11293500","12326175"]`. This session couldn’t directly reopen the Google Patents page itself (blocked to this sandbox), so the new number was cross-confirmed independently instead: multiple separate WebSearch results agree it was granted June 10, 2025 to the same two inventors as the original patent (Michael Macfarland Dulken and Clint John Spiegel, Industry Nine’s founder), under the same F16D 41/30 freewheel/ratchet classification, and covering the newer Hydra2 and Solix hubs alongside the original Hydra. `exp` left at 2038 (the original 2018-filing-derived date) rather than recomputed, since this pass couldn’t confirm whether 12,326,175 is a direct continuation of that same filing (which per this atlas’s continuation rule would keep the same expiry) or an independently later-filed patent (which would have its own, later expiry) — flagged for a future session with working fetch access to resolve. `conf` set to `"m"` for the combined entry (the original 11,293,500 half was `"v"`; the new number’s grant/inventors/classification are solid but its exact filing-date relationship to the original isn’t, so the entry as a whole reflects the weaker-verified half rather than being left at `"v"`). - **Added Shimano’s MICRO SPLINE freehub system, flagged from the start as an unconfirmed patent identification: US 11,578,761 B2.** The user believed this to be Micro Spline’s patent; this session couldn’t independently confirm it — Google Patents and the USPTO record were both blocked to this sandbox, and standalone WebSearch queries for the number turned up nothing at first, then one AI-generated summary that plausibly hallucinated an unrelated match (a self-tightening cycling shoe patent), a failure mode this changelog has now hit on three separate numbers this session (see the Chilao SS and RatioX notes elsewhere in this file) and does not trust on its own. What **did** corroborate it: the user separately supplied the filing date (Nov. 5, 2019, Shimano Inc. applicant) mid-session, and a February 2023 Weight Weenies forum thread, found independently, discusses this same number specifically comparing Microspline/HG+/HG freehub types — real corroboration, just not a primary-source read. Shipped at `conf:"l"` (the user’s own choice, offered a menu of options including holding off entirely) with the gap stated plainly in both the title (“patent number unconfirmed”) and the body text, rather than either omitting a standard this consequential or overstating how solid the identification is. Also notes, as context for the low confidence: cycling trade press (Singletrack World, 2019) reports Shimano has publicly refused licensees’ requests to disclose which patent(s) actually cover Micro Spline — a real reason beyond this session’s own search limits to expect this number to be hard to verify. `g` (grant year) left unset; `st:"active"` set on the strength of the number’s own “B2” kind code (a granted patent, not a pending application) rather than a confirmed grant date; `exp:2039` follows from the confirmed 2019 filing date alone. Placed in `cat:"wheel"` next to Shimano’s original 1978 Freehub cassette hub entry. - **Confirmed the MICRO SPLINE entry (US 11,578,761 B2) from `conf:"l"` to `conf:"v"`, after the user pasted the actual Google Patents page text directly.** Title ("Bicycle sprocket arrangement"), inventor (Tomonari Oka), assignee (Shimano Inc.), filing date (Nov. 5, 2019, Appl. No. 16/673,980, a continuation-in-part of Ser. No. 16/361,249 filed Mar. 22, 2019), grant date (Feb. 14, 2023), and adjusted expiration (Dec. 30, 2040, per Patent Term Adjustment — used directly rather than the plain filing+20 estimate of 2039) are all now read straight from the primary source rather than cross-inferred from secondhand search results, resolving the gap flagged in the previous pass. Rewrote `s`/`w` to describe the actual claimed mechanism now that the full claims were readable: the cassette’s internal spline deliberately mixes two sets of driving-surface positions (nine at a shared 40° pitch plus offset positions) so one cassette can engage two different external-spline hub patterns — the real technical trick behind Micro Spline cross-compatibility, not something the marketing materials spell out. - **Added two new entries: SRAM's XD driver body (`num:null`, `conf:"l"`) and a pending SRAM modular mechanical derailleur application (US 2025/0010943, `conf:"v"`).** XD was introduced with XX1 in 2012 as an open, royalty-free standard licensed to nearly 90 hub manufacturers, per BikeRadar. Its specific patent number wasn't located — searches turned up several candidate SRAM Deutschland cassette/driver filings from the same era, none confirmed as the actual interface patent, so it ships with `num:null` rather than a guess, placed next to Shimano's Micro Spline as the competing freehub standard. US 2025/0010943 A1 is confirmed independently across four sources (Escape Collective, Singletracks, Pinkbike, Brujulabike): filed Sept. 17, 2024 by SRAM Deutschland GmbH, inventors Heiko Redecker and Tobias Harcke, describing a hierarchical modular derailleur construction — read by industry press as groundwork for a mechanical, cable-actuated version of the electronic-only Eagle Transmission. Placed next to the UDH/Transmission cluster. Counts synced: README At-a-glance (284→286 total, 43→44 pending, 172→173 verified, 51→52 draft) and all seven hardcoded `index.html` count strings.

- **Wired in the SRAM modular-derailleur picture** (`pictures/US20250010943A1.png`) onto the US 2025/0010943 entry as `img`/`imgAlt` — a full assembly drawing with reference labels marking the module boundaries (base, swivel, shifting/parallelogram, cage/chain-guide) the entry's text describes, viewed directly before writing the alt text.

- **Fixed a real search bug, reported directly: "SRAM XD" found nothing for the XD driver body entry even though both words are on the card.** The search predicate required the whole typed query to appear as one contiguous substring of the card's text; since a card's title sits before its assignee field in that concatenated text, "xd" and "sram" were never adjacent in that order, so the phrase-level check failed even with both words present. Fixed by splitting the query on whitespace and requiring each word to be found independently, in any order — a general fix, not specific to this one card, since any earlier multi-word search where the matched terms landed in different fields or reversed order was silently failing the same way.

- **Added `SEARCH_SYNONYMS`, a small set of symmetric synonym groups for common component/concept words**, so a search for one term also finds cards that only use a related one — "spoke" finds a card that only says "wheel," and "wheel" finds one that only says "spoke," since both queries expand against the same group rather than one word pointing at the other. See the new "🔍 Search" section above for the current group list. Search-only: it never changes a card's own displayed text or what a category filter chip means.

- **Added `bath` and `plush` to the suspension synonym group** (`suspension`/`shock`/`linkage`/`damper`/`bath`/`plush`) — "bath" catches the Marzocchi portfolio's own "open-bath damping" language, "plush" catches the colloquial ride-feel term riders search for rather than a mechanism name.

- **Added `searchUrl`, a new optional `D` field that overrides a card's auto-built "Search related patents on Google Patents" link with a specific URL.** The auto-built version constructs a query from the assignee and title, which fails for an entry credited under a nickname — Joe Breeze's JBX1 entry is credited `a: "Joe Breeze"`, not his full legal name, so its auto-built query would never have found his own patent filings. Set to `https://patents.google.com/?inventor=Joseph+T.+Breeze` directly. `searchUrl` wins over every other branch of that link's logic when present.

Title, assignee, and inventors cross-confirmed across two independent sources (a Google Patents result and a FreePatentsOnline listing, both quoting the same title/assignee/inventor set); the grant year (2005) is estimated from the patent's position in the USPTO's April 2005 issue-number sequence rather than read directly off the primary record, which this session couldn't reach — flagged in the entry's own text. `exp:2023` (filing+20, already expired), `conf:"m"` for that reason. `cat:"wheel"`, placed next to the XD driver body entry as another hub/rotor-interface patent. Registered `First Principles Performance Products` in `BRANDS`. - **New entry: Chumba Racing's force-channeling rear suspension, three related Ted K. Tanouye filings in one entry.** Chumba (Anaheim, CA, founded 1993 by Tanouye — hand-welded early on out of the back of his SoCal shop, Bike Beat, under the pre-lawsuit name Chumba Wumba) is a small independent builder with real patent coverage behind its frame platform. US 8,136,829, "Force channelling centralization mountain bike and frame," is a related, later-assigned grant refining the same idea with the rocker link on the down tube instead of the seat tube — its priority relationship to the 2008 filing wasn't independently confirmed, so `exp` (2028) is computed only from the confirmed 7,703,788 filing date, not applied to 8,136,829. - **Fixed the entry-count line's start year, and gave card titles more room on phones.** The line under the filters read "294 entries · 1884–2026" after the 1869 Meyer and 1870 Starley & Hillman entries went in, because "1884" was typed into both the English and French strings. It now takes the earliest filing year from the data (`FIRST_YEAR`), so an older entry updates it automatically. On phones (below 640px), each card's year pill and country tag now sit on their own line above the title instead of beside it, so a long title wraps across two or three lines instead of four or five. Desktop layout is unchanged. Checked in headless Chromium at 320px, 390px and 1400px wide; no entry data or counts changed.

- **Stats tab: "Patents by category, over time" moved to the top and made clickable.** The chart now sits directly under the Stats heading, above the four summary boxes. Tapping any colored segment opens the list filtered to that category and scrolls to the first entry in that 5-year bracket (the "<1980" bar scrolls to the category's earliest entry). The bracket sets where the list opens; it doesn't hide other years, so readers can keep scrolling through the rest of that category. Chart intro text updated in both languages to mention the tap. Checked in headless Chromium at 390px and 1400px.

- **Category-over-time chart now cycles between Count and Share on its own.** Every 5 seconds the bars ease between the two views (a 1.2-second height animation; segment proportions don't change between modes, so only bar heights move). The first click on the Count/Share button stops the cycle and keeps the chosen view for the rest of the visit. Readers with "reduce motion" turned on in their device settings get a static chart. Checked in headless Chromium.

- **The Stats tab is now the "Atlas" tab: the first tab and the landing view.** It opens with **Pick a rabbit hole** (three drawing-led story cards), then **"Who invented your bike?"** (a large search box with rotating example placeholders, a live match count as you type, and one-tap "Popular" search chips with live counts), four **invitation cards** that replace the old number boxes and each open their list, the clickable category chart, **Start from a brand** chips, and the original statistics under "Atlas by the numbers." Two new registries sit after `FIGHTS`: `RABBIT_HOLES` (hand-written cards, each with a title, a teaser and a `go` target, which is a card, a search, a filter or a tab; `pin:true` keeps one always shown) and `HOME_SEARCH_SUGGESTIONS`. `RABBIT_HOLES` ships empty, so the row is fully automatic until curated cards are added. The internal tab key stays `stats`, so existing `#tab=stats` links still work. Older links with filters but no `tab=` still open on the Category tab. Typing in the main search box from the Atlas or Fights tab now moves to the full list with filters cleared; before, it did nothing visible there. On phones, the header's era scrubber is hidden on the Atlas tab only, so the page's content starts higher up; the category chart covers the same ground. New chrome strings are in both languages; the French is AI-drafted, like the rest of the interface.
- **Search fix: "dropper" no longer matches unrelated entries.** The `SEARCH_SYNONYMS` group for dropper posts included the bare word "post", which matched "post-GATT", "in post" and French words like "postérieur", adding 10 unrelated cards to any dropper or seatpost search (27 results fell to 18). The group is now `["dropper","seatpost"]`.

- **Atlas tab "Popular" chips: "Boost spacing" swapped for "Modular" (5 matches), plus one rotating chip.** A new `HOME_SEARCH_ROTATING` registry holds five keywords: Wireless (11), E-bikes (12), Disc brakes (15), Narrow-wide (7) and Hitch racks (8). They were chosen because their results stay on topic; "carbon" and "air spring" were tried and rejected because their results drifted into unrelated cards. The rotating chip sits first in the row, has a dashed border, and fades to the next keyword every 4 seconds, starting on a random one each visit. It pauses while hovered or focused so the label can't change under a tap, stays still under "reduce motion", and is sized to its longest keyword so the chips after it don't shift.

- **Atlas tab: the four invitation cards (still in force, expired, went to court, expiring soon) moved below "Most-patented names,"** just above the Expiring soon list. Markup reorder only; behavior unchanged.

- **SE Quadangle entry now names BMX.** Its summary (both languages) now identifies the Quadangle as SE Racing's BMX race frame and ties it to early BMX racing, so a "BMX" search finds it. Wording only; tier (`l`), dates and other fields unchanged.

- **Schwinn Sting-Ray banana seat entry now connects it to BMX.** One sentence added to its "why it matters" text (both languages): the Sting-Ray was BMX's first race bike, raced by kids on Southern California dirt tracks in the late 1960s, before purpose-built BMX frames existed. Consistent with Britannica's and Wikipedia's BMX histories (the first organized race was at Palms Park, Santa Monica, in 1969). Tier (`v`) unchanged; the addition is history, not a patent claim.

- **New entry: e*thirteen Sidekick anti-kickback hub (US 12,643,343).** "Hub system, method and device with adjustable deadband," filed 2023 by The Hive Global (e*thirteen's parent); also published as WO 2024/107631 and US 2024/0157728, all three linked on the card. A freehub with a deliberate, adjustable deadband that absorbs chain growth so suspension compression doesn't kick back through the pedals, and can be set wide enough to coast silently. Tier `v`: the grant number comes from e*thirteen's own patent-marking page, and the WO/US publications, assignee and inventor are consistent across multiple search results. The marking page and Google Patents are blocked from this environment, so the 2026 grant year is inferred from the patent's number range and should be checked directly. Drawing (`pictures/WO2024107631A1.png`) not yet wired in. Counts synced: 294→295 total, 136→137 active, 183→184 verified.

- **SE Quadangle entry rewritten by Jon.** New summary puts the Quadangle in context: SE Racing's aluminum PK Ripper (1978) came first and disrupted a chromoly-dominated BMX scene, and the Quadangle followed in 1979. The new "why it matters" frames it as a monument to an era of garage welders working free of IP concerns. This replaces the "uncopyrighted" line; the frame question was always patent, not copyright. PK Ripper's 1978 launch in aluminum matches SE Racing histories. Those same histories say the Quadangle design began slightly earlier as the STR-1, built for Stu Thomsen, so "one year later" holds for the Quadangle name rather than the design. - **Added a new suspension entry: Propain's PRO10 floating-shock linkage (DE 10 2008 011 364 A1).** German published application "Bicycle frame has soft tail...", filed by founder/engineer Robert Krauss and published from Propain Bicycles, describing a rear triangle driven by two seesaw links pivoting between the main and rear frame — the mechanism behind Propain's PRO10 rear end. Propain's own support material (independently found and quoted in search results) describes PRO10 as four pivot points driving a floating shock squeezed from both ends by two counter-rotating links, which matches this document's drawing closely enough to treat as the same mechanism; used that description for the entry's `w` text. **Two things this pass could not verify, both stated honestly in the entry rather than guessed:** a second, earlier Krauss filing (reported in the sourcing material as DE 10 2006 042 165 A1, filed September 2006, possibly this design's direct parent) could not be independently confirmed to exist — Google Patents itself is blocked to this session and no search result corroborated that specific number, so it's named as an unconfirmed lead rather than linked as a related filing; and this document's own prosecution status is unconfirmed — "A1" marks an unexamined published application, not a grant, and no grant record was found, so `st:"unknown"`/`exp:null` rather than assuming it issued. Ships `conf:"m"` on that basis. Image: Fig. 1 (user-supplied, already in `pictures/`), showing the counter-rotating seesaw links (6, 11) squeezing the shock spring (4). Registered `Propain` in `BRANDS` and `BRAND_HQ` (Germany); Robert Krauss credited in `a` prose only, not added to `INVENTORS`, matching the atlas's "recurring figure only" rule — this is his only entry so far. - **Added a new suspension entry: Mondraker's ZERO floating-shock suspension system, `num: null`.** Introduced in 2009 on the Dune, developed with engineer Cesar Rojo — credited as ZERO's designer by Mondraker's own site and multiple interviews, one of which describes the patent as held personally by Rojo rather than by Mondraker. The mechanism (two short links, both shock eyelets mounted to the links rather than one end fixed to the front triangle, so the shock compresses from both ends) and the "Four Zeros" marketing claims (Zero Power Loss, Zero Pedal Kickback, Zero Brake Jack, Zero Bumps) are both sourced to Mondraker's own product page. **The specific patent number is the gap this entry is honest about:** three candidates were supplied in the request for this entry — US 2018/0099723 A1, its WO 2018/065096 A1 international publication, and a Spanish filing cited as ES 2,377,317 — and none of the three turned up in any independent search result across roughly a dozen queries, nor could any be directly verified, since Google Patents, Espacenet, WIPO PatentScope and Justia's patent search are all blocked to this session's network. Rather than pick one candidate to store as `num` on the strength of the request alone, this entry ships `num: null` with all three numbers named in the entry's own text as unconfirmed leads for a future pass with working patent-database access — the same honest-gap handling as the EU RCD and pre-1916 GB conventions already documented in CLAUDE.md, applied here because no candidate could be confirmed rather than because the format doesn't fit. - **Worked a four-candidate list surfaced from bicycleretailer.com; added one, found two already covered, and skipped one as not genuinely MTB-relevant (314 → 315 entries).** Added Chris King's internally cable-routed **AeroSet headset (US 12,337,923 B2)**, whose own PDF is a pure image scan with no extractable text layer, so its front page (filing date, named inventor(s)) could not be read this pass; the patent number itself is cross-confirmed across two independent trade outlets (Bicycle Retailer, Pinkbike) reporting Chris King's own July 2025 grant announcement, and its Fig. 3 (cable routed through the enlarged top cap and bearing stack into the steerer) was sourced via the `Fetch patent figure` workflow and used as `img`. Ships `conf:"m"`: the mechanism, product line and patent number are solid, but the filing year (`y:2021`) is inferred only from reporting that AeroSet shipped "patent pending" for several years before the 2025 grant, not read off the document — flagged in the entry's own `s` text as approximate pending a session with working patent-database access. Genuinely MTB-relevant: internal cable routing through the headset bearings is now common on modern MTB frames, and the entry sits next to the existing GripLock (US 8,662,517) and Intend Stiffmaster headset entries. **Two of the four candidates were already in the dataset and were not duplicated:** Trek's IsoSpeed decoupler (already catalogued, `num:null`, `conf:"m"` — the specific number reported, US 10,086,899, was not merged in, since the existing entry already correctly notes IsoSpeed's crossover to MTB hardtails like the Procaliber and upgrading its `num`/dates was out of scope for this pass) and Trek's rim-development-method patent (already catalogued as US 10,733,795, `conf:"v"`, under the same title reported by Bicycle Retailer's 2020 coverage of the earlier-published application 2019/0213785). **One candidate skipped as not genuinely MTB-relevant:** NULL WINDS' Streamlined Tapered Bicycle Wheel Spoke (US 10,773,543, confirmed real and correctly numbered) is aerodynamic fairing/spoke-profile technology for reducing crosswind drag — built for road and time-trial wheels, with no off-road application or MTB market presence found in any source consulted; adding it under the `wheel` category would have implied an MTB relevance the patent itself doesn't have, so it was left out rather than forced in. Registries: no new `BRANDS`/`INVENTORS` needed (Chris King already registered). - **Added two PI ROPE GmbH braided-fiber spoke filings, one a granted German patent and one a German utility model (315 → 317 entries).** **DE 10 2017 116 754 B4**, "Spoke based on high-performance fibers for a system impeller and system impeller," a granted (kind code B4) German patent held by PI ROPE GmbH, founded by Ingo Berbig in Chemnitz: a limp, braided high-performance-fiber (Vectran) spoke connecting hub to rim through a clamping-tensioned end connection, with a claimed cross-section that's square or rectangular untensioned and rounds or ovals under tension. **DE 20 2017 104 416 U1** covers closely related claims on the same underlying technology as a Gebrauchsmuster — a German utility model registered without substantive examination, a real but weaker instrument than an examined patent, the same distinction already drawn for Intend's Stiffmaster headset entry; kept the `U1` suffix on `num` per the schema's own convention for this format. Both entries' primary source is the patent's own text and abstract on its Google Patents record, supplied directly by the user rather than fetched by this session (patents.google.com remains blocked to this sandbox's egress); ship `conf:"m"` on that basis, not `"v"`, since the numbers themselves weren't independently pulled by this session even though the supplied text is a legitimate primary source. **Filing-date discrepancy, stated honestly in both entries rather than picked silently:** independent trade coverage (veloheld.com, Bikerumor) and TU Chemnitz's own account both place Berbig's initial work on the textile-spoke idea in 2016, while the "DE102017"/"DE202017" number prefixes formally encode a 2017 application year under Germany's numbering convention; `y:2016` was used as the better-corroborated year, with the conflict named in each entry's `s` text for a future pass with working patent-database access to resolve. `exp:2036` on the B4 patent follows the post-GATT filing+20 rule from that 2016 filing year; the U1 utility model instead carries `st:"unknown"`/`exp:null`, since a Gebrauchsmuster's renewal-paid term isn't the fixed 20-year patent clock this atlas's `exp` formula assumes and this pass has no confirmation the registration's renewal fees have been kept current. Grant date on the B4 patent is unconfirmed and left `g:null` rather than guessed — the supplied source text states the patent was granted (the B4 kind code itself denotes that) but not on what date. **No images**: checked `tools/fetch_patent_figure.py` before attempting a fetch — it hardcodes a `US`-prefixed number format throughout (`parse_number`'s regex and its `"US%s%s%s"` URL construction) and has no path for a `DE`-prefixed number, so the `Fetch patent figure` GitHub Actions workflow can't be run against either entry; both ship without `img`/`imgAlt` rather than a fabricated or hand-placed figure. Registered `PI ROPE` in `BRANDS` and `BRAND_HQ` (Saxony, Germany, confirmed via independent search corroborating Chemnitz as the company's base). Placed both entries in the `wheel` category directly after the Berd LLC spoke-patent cluster, since they're independently developed but directly comparable braided-fiber spoke technology — noted the comparison in the B4 entry's `w` text. - **Added an optional `long` field for a second, deeper narrative layer, and a "Read the full story →" disclosure to show it.** Prompted by a queued entry (Spurcycle's bell) with more well-sourced material — a patent's own Background section naming an earlier unpatented product, plus two independent company-history articles — than `s`/`w` should carry without either bloating every reader's default card view or leaving real substance out. `long` renders as a native `<details>` element at the bottom of the card body, the same zero-JS-to-open pattern this file already uses for the multi-number `.gp-list` block and the share menu, so it costs nothing on the 321 entries that don't have one and needed no new JavaScript to open/close. Styled with the same theme CSS variables as `.gp-list` (`--primary`, `--card-border`), so it matches all four visual themes automatically rather than needing its own per-theme rule. This is deliberately an escape valve, not a new default — CLAUDE.md's schema section now says so explicitly, so a future pass doesn't start padding ordinary entries with it just because the field exists. No entry currently sets `long`; the Spurcycle entry (still a queued task as of this writing) will be the first to use it. No count changes.

A thumb lever spins a spring-loaded striker carrier inside the bell; the striker, longer than its carrier, has one end always projecting to strike the dome, ringing twice per rotation. Registered `Lucas` in `BRANDS`/`BRAND_HQ` (England); Alfred Nicholas stays in `a` prose only, per the one-off-inventor convention. Ships `conf:"v"` — number, dates, inventor, assignee and mechanism all confirmed against the patent's own front page and specification text. **Added Spurcycle's later strike-lever mechanism patent (TWI 725,811 B), using the new `long` field for the first time.** Filed as a U.S. non-provisional on March 18, 2020 (on an April 2019 provisional), granted in Taiwan April 21, 2021, credited to Clinton N. Slone, Nicholaus M. Slone and Adam R.L. Reineck of Slone Studio Inc. — names and Sausalito address confirmed directly from the corresponding U.S. publication's own front page (US 2020/0327874 A1), which this pass could not confirm has since granted under its own number. The patent's own background section names the original 2013 Spurcycle bell directly as prior art before presenting a different mechanism (a spring-loaded strike lever inside the bell cavity) as the improvement — that original bell was never itself covered by a design patent, the actual point of the entry. `s`/`w` stay on the core IP story (the AIA first-to-file gap that left Spurcycle with no recourse against counterfeiters like Rock Brothers); `long` carries the fuller account: the counterfeit timeline in the Slones' own words, the material/engineering reason the tone was worth copying, and the patent-background-as-admission-against-interest angle, plus the open question of whether a "early 2017" redesign Clint Slone described to Velo in 2016 is the same hardware generation as this 2020 filing. Registered `Spurcycle`, `Clint Slone` and `Nick Slone` in `BRANDS`/`INVENTORS`/`BRAND_HQ` (California); Adam Reineck, credited only on this later filing, stays in `a` prose per the one-off-contributor convention. - **A review of the ~40 most recently added entries found exactly two without pictures — both PI ROPE GmbH's German-numbered filings — because `tools/fetch_patent_figure.py` only ever parsed US patent numbers. Extended the tool to accept a non-US office prefix** (`parse_number()` now falls through to a second pattern for a number like `DE102017116754B4`; `download_pdf()` skips the US-only USPTO print endpoint and its US-specific kind-code-guessing loop for a non-US canonical, trusting the given kind code instead of guessing across an office's own scheme), added self-test coverage for the new path, then ran the `Fetch patent figure` GitHub Actions workflow against both numbers and wired in drawings. **Both entries' own front pages, read for the first time this pass, resolved more than the missing pictures.** DE 10 2017 116 754 B4: confirmed filed July 25, 2017 and granted (published) May 25, 2023, naming five inventors (Ingo Berbig, Klaus Nendel, Daniela Storch, Dirk Fischer, Stephanie Mager) where the entry previously credited Berbig alone. The prior pass's flagged "2016 vs. 2017" filing-year discrepancy is resolved by the front page itself: this application claims internal priority (*innere Priorität*) from an earlier July 25, 2016 German filing, but a priority claim shifts novelty assessment only — the term clock runs from the application's own filing date under German law, same as US/Paris Convention practice — so `y`/`exp` move to 2017/2037 rather than the 2016/2036 the prior pass had used. Tier moves `m` → `v` now that the front page itself, not a supplied secondhand text, is the source. DE 20 2017 104 416 U1 (the companion Gebrauchsmuster): confirmed filed the same day, registered October 26, 2017, published December 7, 2017. Brings its `st`/`exp` treatment in line with this atlas's existing Intend Stiffmaster precedent for a German utility model (`st:"active"`, `exp` = filing + 10 = 2027) rather than the `unknown`/`null` the prior pass used before that precedent was confirmed against this document; tier moves `m` → `v` for the same reason as its companion. Added two images to the B4 entry (Fig. 1, the assembled wheel; Figs. 2–3, the rim- and hub-end spoke terminations) and one to the U1 entry (its own copy of Fig. 1 — the two filings' drawings are otherwise identical). No entry count change (330 total, both entries already existed). Counts synced: active 146 → 147, unknown 20 → 19, verified 220 → 222, medium confidence 65 → 63; expired, pending, litigated, brands, inventors and jurisdictions unchanged. README's "At a glance" table updated; the six hardcoded `index.html` count strings weren't touched since the total didn't change.
- **Merged two independent sessions' work on the same queued candidate list, which had converged on 5 of the same inventions (and, in Rolf Dietrich's and Raphael Schlanger's cases, the identical patent number) without either session knowing about the other — 337 + 330 entries collapsed to 338, not 343.** Dropped this session's own duplicate entries for Spinergy Rev-X, Rolf's paired-spoke wheel, Giro's helmet, and Browning's sprocket transmission in favor of the other session's versions, which were better-sourced in every case (Rolf's, especially, correctly traces a three-generation continuation chain to an original 1991 filing that this session's version had missed entirely, using a 1997 continuation's own date instead — moving `exp` from 2017 to the correct 2016). Look's clipless pedal wasn't an exact duplicate but the same invention under two different real, related patent numbers (this session had the continuation, US 4,882,946; the other had the parent, US 4,762,019) — kept as two separate entries rather than merged into one, since both are legitimate documents in the same family and the parent is the more foundational one. **The most consequential catch: the other session's independent research found that this session's Kestrel entry had the story wrong.** It credited Brent Trimble and US 4,982,975 with the Kestrel 4000 itself; the other session traced the bike's actual frame patent to Kestrel co-founders Delano Duplessis and Bevil Hogg (US 4,828,781) and added Trimble's own, separately-credited "X-Frame" design (US 4,923,203) as a different bike entirely. Rewrote this entry's `t`/`s`/`w` to correct the record — Trimble's US 4,982,975 remains in the atlas as a real, verified Cycle Composites manufacturing-technique patent, just no longer mis-credited as the Kestrel 4000's own frame patent — and used the `w` field to name the error and how it was caught, since a mistake compounding two sessions' worth of research into an actual publication is worth being visible about rather than quietly fixed. Registries, `img`/`imgAlt` and the remaining 8 entries from this session's batch (Kestrel's manufacturing patent itself, Browning is dropped/replaced above, Boone Lennon, Mavic Zap, Look's continuation, V-Brake, Biopace, Reisinger, Magic Motorcycle, Mantis) all carried over untouched. a re-fetch at a higher page offset is a job for a future pass, not this one).** Wired drawings onto five entries: the Tower wrench design (USD 20,932, Fig. 1/2, the flat-sided handle profile); the WickWerks chainring continuation (US 10,711,875, Fig. 1, the four ramp profiles); the Mongoose Motomag wheel (US D236,609, Figs. 1–2 from the patent's own front page); the Sotto/Praxis Levatime sprocket (US 2012/0214629, Fig. 1, the two-radius tooth geometry); and the myStromer/Fairly Bike motor hub (EP 2,546,134, Fig. 2, the hub's exploded stack) — all confirmed against their own front pages before writing bilingual `imgAlt`. **Reading US 10,711,875's own front page in full (its "Related U.S. Application Data" section runs onto a second page a prior pass evidently didn't reach) found the entry's stated continuation lineage was wrong**: it does not stop at a 2011 parent as previously stated, it continues through the same chain as the 10,677,338 entry beside it, back to the identical April 3, 2006 non-provisional parent. `exp` corrected 2031 → 2026 to match; `s`/`w` rewritten to state the corrected chain plainly rather than repeat the disproven "separate lineage" framing. **Two of the four foreign-numbered entries this review initially flagged as blocked by the fetch tool's now-since-fixed US-only parsing turned out, once actually fetched, to be the wrong patent entirely** — the same failure mode CLAUDE.md's own sourcing-discipline section already documents for this atlas (a plausible-looking secondhand number that isn't the right document): the BMX freecoaster entry's EP number (EP 1,308,337) is a Toyoda Koki/Mazda four-wheel-drive power-distribution patent, and the KOM Xeno Hub entry's WO number (WO2025/003104) is an unrelated video-compression patent. Hammond personally, with "North Shore Racks Inc." only a separately recorded Google Patents "current assignee" — corrected from the prior pass's implied joint assignment. **Trek's OCLV patent (US 6,924,021) was actually filed July 3, 2002, not August 22, 2002** as one of the two conflicting web searches in the original entry had suggested; corrected (no change to `exp`, same filing year either way). **Kuat's rack patent** (US 10,668,866, filed Sept. 2017, granted June 2020) and **Pinion's gearbox patent** (US 8,608,610) both moved from `num:null`/`conf:"m"` placeholders to real numbers at `conf:"v"` — Pinion's case needed its own reasoning spelled out in the entry: the patent is a continuation of PCT/EP2009/009193, so its U.S. 20-year term runs from that application's December 21, 2009 international filing date, not the later June 2011 U.S. national-phase entry date, moving `exp` from an estimated 2030 to a confirmed 2029. **Shimano's "compact drive unit" entry moved from `st:"pending"` to a real granted patent**, US 11,807,338 (filed Jan. 2020, granted Nov. 2023), with all four named inventors now credited. **Bosch's eBike ABS entry's dates, previously estimated and tiered `conf:"m"`, are now primary-source-confirmed** (filed Sept. 8, 2020, granted June 21, 2022 — matching the prior estimate almost exactly) and upgraded to `conf:"v"`. - **Six new entries added via the undirected batch-sourcing workflow (338 → 354, following a merge of an intervening upstream fix that isn't otherwise noted here), targeting the atlas's thinnest categories — transport, tech, frame — plus a genuinely uncovered gap in a thick one (Cannondale had zero `susp` entries despite being a major full-suspension brand).** A research pass surfaced 12 candidates; cross-checking each against the existing 348-entry dataset (not just the brand list) found that three of them — Kuat's rack patent, Pinion's gearbox patent, and Shimano's compact-drive-unit patent — would only have filled in real numbers on `num:null` placeholder entries already in the atlas, not created new cards, so they were held back as a separate, not-yet-actioned opportunity rather than added or discarded. Rather than drop it, it's added correctly attributed: Mert Lawwill already has one entry in this atlas (his four-bar Gary Fisher RS-1 linkage) and Schwinn is already a registered brand, so no new registrations were needed, and the entry's own `w` text names the misattribution risk directly (a no-pivot flexible-stay suspension resurfaced commercially as Cannondale's own FlexPivot two decades later, but nothing checked this pass ties FlexPivot's actual patent to this specific 1998 filing — noted as a real design lineage, not a confirmed shared patent). Ships `conf:"v"` — number, dates and inventors all cross-confirmed via Google Patents. **The five other new entries, all cross-confirmed against at least two independent sources unless noted:** North Shore Racks' foundational fork-crown-supported hitch carrier (US 7,900,802, filed Feb. 2004 by co-founder Malcolm T. Hammond four months after the North Vancouver company launched, granted March 2011, now expired) — a new brand, registered in `BRANDS`/`BRAND_HQ` (British Columbia, Canada); Trek's foundational OCLV carbon-manufacturing patent (US 6,924,021, filed Aug. 2002, granted Aug. 2005, now expired) — the atlas's first entry on how a carbon MTB frame is actually built rather than its geometry; Bosch's eBike ABS pressure-modulator (US 11,364,968, assignee and inventor Alessandro Moia confirmed via Google Patents, but exact filing/grant dates could not be independently confirmed this pass — patents.google.com stayed unreachable from this sandbox, so the 2020/2022 dates given are estimated from the patent's own number sequence and Bosch's product-launch timing, not read off the document; ships `conf:"m"` for that reason); and Fox Factory's Dual Live Valve rear shock (US 12,253,139, filed Sept. 2020, granted March 2025, cross-confirmed via Justia and Google Patents), a later, `susp`-side refinement placed alongside the existing fork-side Live Valve bundle rather than folded into it, since it's a distinct, newer filing covering independent main-damper/base-valve control. **One entry shipped as an honest `num:null` gap rather than a guess:** Specialized's ANGi helmet crash-detection sensor (accelerometer/gyroscope, Bluetooth-paired, auto-texts an emergency contact) — the ICEdot acquisition (2017–18) and 2018 ANGi launch are well-documented across independent outlets, but no patent number could be located despite Specialized's own marketing calling the system patented; ships `conf:"l"`. - **Six new entries added via the undirected batch-sourcing workflow, curated and sourced entirely by Claude at the user's explicit request (354 → 360).** A research pass surfaced roughly 20 candidates across a dozen leads; screening each against the existing 354-entry dataset by number, title, and assignee found that most were already covered by prior sessions — Fox's Dual Live Valve, Garmin's camera-augmented radar, Chris King's Aeroset internal-routing headset, Dave Weagle's dual-chain high-pivot drivetrain, DT Swiss's Star Ratchet, Specialized's SWAT storage and UST tubeless system among them — and several more real, documented inventions (Wolf Tooth's ReMote dropper lever, Tannus Armour tire inserts, Litelok's flexible lock, Squirt's wax lube, a disputed 1930 Campagnolo quick-release patent) turned up no confirmable, independently-sourced patent number and were left out rather than guessed. Lewicki, pre-GATT term so `exp` runs grant+17) — the foundational patent behind the Bell Biker, placed next to the existing modern Bell Sports roll-cage entry as the same company's earlier chapter; the NuVinci continuously-variable planetary hub's foundational patent (US 6,241,636, filed 1998, granted 2001, assignee Motion Technologies LLC — the company later doing business as Fallbrook Technologies and, today, Enviolo) — ships `conf:"m"` since this single number is one piece of a dozen-plus-patent family not individually re-verified this pass, a new brand; and Specialized's Mirror 3D-printed lattice saddle (US 11,186,336, filed 2020, granted 2021) — concentric variable-stiffness regions manufactured via Carbon's Digital Light Synthesis process. **Images fetched via the `Fetch patent figure` GitHub Actions workflow for all six** (the first run 403'd/503'd on every patent — even the runner's own egress was rate-limited that moment — a same-input retry cleared on the second attempt); candidate sheets were reviewed and a figure picked for each directly by Claude rather than queued for the user's sign-off, per the user's explicit "curated by Claude" instruction for this batch — an intentional, flagged exception to this project's normal figure-selection-is-Jon's-call convention, worth a spot-check on the next review pass. Every front-page report was read and cross-checked against the entry text before writing it (confirming, among other things, that Bell's assignee is "Bell Helmets Inc.," credited under the modern "Bell Sports" `who[]` key with the naming difference stated in prose). New registrations: `BRANDS` gains Fidlock, Burley, and NuVinci; no new `INVENTORS` entries (all named inventors stay in `a` prose only, per the existing one-off-engineer convention). - **One new entry, patent number supplied directly by the user: ThyssenKrupp Bilstein's amplitude-dependent bicycle-fork damper (US 2024/0149970 A1, application, 360 → 361).** Jon pasted the Google Patents record directly — title, abstract, inventors (Michael Heyer, Hanno Luczak, Dennis Gundlach, Daniel Hansen), assignee (ThyssenKrupp AG / ThyssenKrupp Bilstein GmbH), application number, filing date (Oct. 27, 2023, claiming a 2022 German priority), and publication date (May 9, 2024) — so this skipped straight to the single-entry workflow rather than the undirected batch-sourcing one. Still a published application, not a granted patent, so `st:"pending"` and `exp:null`; ships `conf:"v"` since the bibliographic data came from the primary Google Patents record itself rather than secondhand coverage. Describes a damper with amplitude-dependent damping: a parallel compensation cylinder, split by its own separating piston into two part-spaces, each connected through a throttle device to one of the main damper's two damping spaces — a car-suspension-style approach (tuning damping by displacement amplitude, not just velocity) from a major automotive/motorsport damper maker, applied to a bicycle fork under ThyssenKrupp's own name for the first time. Placed in the `fork` category next to the existing Öhlins TTX entry, another automotive-motorsport-heritage damper brand crossing into MTB. Jon had already uploaded both patent-drawing sheets via GitHub's web UI before this session started; used both as an `imgs[]` pair — Fig. 1 (the damper mechanism cross-section, sheet 1 of 3) as the primary image, and the whole-fork-leg elevation view as context, the same two-image pattern this atlas uses elsewhere (e.g. Split Pivot's bearing-stack-plus-whole-linkage pair) when one figure shows the mechanism and another shows where it sits in the finished product. New `BRANDS` registration: ThyssenKrupp Bilstein. - **Two small UI fixes, requested directly: a missing scroll arrow on the Category filter row, and a new sort-order toggle.** The Category row (`rowCat`) was the only one of the five filter-tab rows never wrapped in the `.scroller` structure that gives Inventors/Brands their fade-edge and left/right arrow buttons — it still scrolled (every `.frow` does, via touch/wheel), but with no visual affordance that there was more to see, so its overflow past nine categories read as a dead end rather than a scrollable list. Wrapped it in the same `.scroller`/`setupScroller("scrollCat","rowCat")` pattern Inventors and Brands already use; Status and Story weren't touched since neither currently overflows. Also swapped the scroll arrows' old `‹`/`›` text glyphs for Lucide chevron-left/chevron-right SVGs (requested), across all three scrollable rows for consistency, sized to match the existing circular button via a new `.scroll-btn svg` rule. Separately, added a sort-order toggle button to `.fmeta-actions` (left of Surprise me/Share, as requested), using the requested Lucide clock-arrow-up/clock-arrow-down icons: one button that flips between "Oldest first" (the existing default — filed-year ascending, unchanged) and "Newest first," swapping its own icon/label/title on click. Implemented as a new `sortDesc` state var read by `render()`'s existing sort call and by a reversed copy of `getEras()`'s bucket order when descending, so the era-grouped section layout stays intact in either direction rather than needing a second render path. Doesn't persist across a reload, matching the Stats tab's existing Count/Share toggle. Visually confirmed via Playwright screenshots at mobile (420px, where the category overflow is worst) and desktop width, in both the default WMBC theme and CAMBA, plus a French-language check that the sort button's label re-localizes correctly through the existing `applyStaticI18n()` pass. No data, schema, or count changes.

Applies to all three scrollable filter rows (Category, Inventors, Brands) since they share one CSS rule. Visually confirmed at mobile width.
- **Merged the separate "Switch Technology" and "Sine Suspension" entries into one, per Jon's own confirmation that they're the same patent family (361 → 360).** Jon pointed directly to Google Patents' record for US 8,066,297 B2 and confirmed the two entries had been describing one eccentric-pivot mechanism under two brand names rather than two separate inventions — Sotto Group licensed it to Yeti as Switch, then, after reassigning the family to Luther M. Beale and David M. Earle's own Sine Suspension LLC in 2020, it's now licensed to Alchemy Bikes as Sine. The prior Sine entry had already flagged this exact overlap as an unresolved "worth flagging" note (Sotto's patent notice lists "Sine" against the same four-filing family cited on the Switch entry); Jon's direction resolves that open question rather than introducing a new claim. - **Added a Favorites feature: a heart toggle on every card, and a Favorites tab that appears once something's actually been saved.** Requested directly, prototyped first as a standalone artifact so the interaction could be tried before touching this file, then built in against this tool's real architecture rather than the prototype's own simplified one. Each card's header row gained a third icon button (`card-fav-btn`, alongside the existing share/cite buttons) using two Lucide icons supplied directly — heart-plus for "not yet saved," a filled heart for "saved" — toggling a `favorites` Set of stable `cardRef()` strings, persisted to `localStorage` (`pma-favorites`) since this is a static, backend-less, no-account tool and per-browser storage is the only option available; every read/write is wrapped in try/catch, since this tool also runs inside a cross-origin iframe on postmillenniumrenaissance.com where some browsers restrict storage for an embedded third-party origin. The Favorites tab doesn't exist in the tab row at all until the first heart is tapped (matching the feature request's own wording), then animates in and persists across reloads for a returning visitor with saved entries. Deliberately *not* built as a custom view like Fights/Stats: it's one added line in the existing `passes()` filter predicate (`if(activeTab==="fav" && !isFav(cardRef(d))) return false`), so the Favorites tab reuses the exact same card rendering, search box, and "Surprise me" pool as every other tab — searching while viewing Favorites narrows within your saved set, and Surprise Me there only ever picks a favorited entry, both for free rather than as separately-built behavior. Two defensive fixes made along the way: the result-count line (`"N of 359 entries"` vs. the full-dataset line) now accounts for `activeTab==="fav"`, since `anyFilterActive()` alone can't see it and was about to print the misleading full-dataset count on an empty-filters Favorites view; and `jumpToCard()`'s existing "card not found, reset and retry" fallback now also exits the Favorites tab, closing a latent gap where a deep link to a non-favorited entry while already on that tab would have silently failed to find its target. Bilingual from the start (`tabFavorites`/`favAdd`/`favRemove`/`favEmpty` in both `T.en`/`T.fr`). No `D`/registry changes, so no count sync needed. Verified with a dedicated Playwright script (not committed — this tool has no test suite per this file's own convention) exercising the full flow: tab absent on a fresh visit, appears on first favorite, button and result-count reflect the saved state, the tab's own list shows exactly the favorited card, state survives a reload, and un-favoriting from within the tab empties it with a dedicated message rather than the generic "no results" text — zero JS errors across the whole run. `verify_data.js` and the standard `smoke_test.js` both still pass clean at the unchanged 359-entry baseline.
- **Added a new entry for Peaty's Holeshot CO2 tyre inflator valve (GB 2,615,585, verified, 359 → 360) — and deliberately did *not* apply this patent number to the existing Fast Flow tubeless valve entry.** Jon pasted Google Patents' full record for GB2615585A believing it covered the Holeshot Fast Flow valve. Its own abstract — two body parts sliding against a spring, an egress aperture that clears a pair of O-ring seals, a fitting for a compressed-gas cylinder — describes a CO2-inflator mechanism, not the Fast Flow's rim-integrated "bell house" seal; cross-checked against Peaty's own product pages and press coverage of both product lines, which confirmed they're mechanically unrelated products sharing only a brand and a "Holeshot" name prefix. Added as its own entry (inventor Morgan Bryn, assignee Peatys Ltd, filed February 2022, granted June 2025, `exp` 2042) instead, leaving the Fast Flow entry's own still-pending, `conf:"l"` status untouched. The EUIPO registered community design Jon also flagged, EM86258340002S (filed July 2021 by Peaty's Limited), couldn't be matched to either product from the information available this pass — RCDs carry no text abstract to check against, so it's left out rather than guessed at; a future session with a way to view the actual registered design image should resolve which Peaty's product it covers.
- **Added a new entry for Canyon's magnetic steering stabilizer (EP 4,613,621, application, 360 → 361).** Jon supplied Google Patents' record directly: a diametrically magnetized ring magnet fixed to the fork steerer and a matching one fixed to the head tube generate a centering restoring torque with no physical spring or damper touching the steerer. Filed as EP25162497.9 on March 10, 2025, claiming priority from a 2024 German filing; still pending, no grant yet. Placed in the `fork` category next to the existing Rimpact tuned-mass-damper entry — a different technology aimed at the same steering-wander problem.
- **Added a new entry for a Shimano pedal-body load-spacing patent (US 11,858,587, verified, 361 → 362).** Jon supplied the number directly. Filed October 2020, granted January 2024 to Shimano Inc.; covers a pedal shaft/body interface where the load-receiving part sits slightly clear of the shaft's contact portion until a rider's weight is actually applied, rather than carrying constant preload the way a conventional bushing or bearing does. Placed directly after the SPD clipless-pedal entry in the existing pedal cluster.

The entry previously described a single high-pivot design; it's actually covered by three separate pending applications — a third-member-link anti-rise system, a dynamic belt/chain growth tensioner (needed because a Gates belt can't stretch or flex the way a chain can), and a pedal-kickback-neutralization layout — none of which has a published U.S. number yet. Also corrected: the platform was previously (wrongly) described as a true single pivot; the third-member link means it isn't. `a` field now also credits Michael Schwartz of Morpheus Cycles, named directly as the developer Priority partnered with.

- **Corrected the pre-GATT patent-term rule, in `CLAUDE.md` and in the data.** The contributor guide stated the pre-June-8-1995 rule as a flat `grant year + 17`. That is only half of 35 U.S.C. §154(c): a patent still in force on June 8, 1995 gets the **greater** of grant+17 and filing+20 ([MPEP 2701](https://www.uspto.gov/web/offices/pac/mpep/s2701.html)). Taking grant+17 by reflex understates every patent that issued less than three years after it was filed, which is most of them. The guide also claimed the pre-1995 case "has mattered exactly three times so far (the Schwinn entries)"; the real figure is 57 single-patent entries. Both statements are now rewritten, and the rule is spelled out as the three distinct sub-cases it actually is — patents alive on the 1995 cutoff (greater-of), patents that had already expired by then (plain grant+17, the law of their own day, so the greater-of test must *not* be applied), and design patents, which sit outside §154(c) entirely and whose pre-1982 terms were **elected** by the applicant at 3½/7/14 years and so cannot be computed from a grant year at all.

- **24 `exp` values corrected as a consequence**, all on single-number, non-design, pre-1995 utility entries: RockShox RS-1 2007→2009, SPD 2009→2011, CamelBak 2008→2010, Trimble composite joint 2006→2009, Cool Tool 2007→2010, Boone Lennon aero bar 2005→2007, Cunningham roller-cam 2005→2007, Time pedal 2006→2008, Softride beam 2007→2009, Yamaha pedal-force assist 2011→2013, Spinergy Rev-X 2009→2010, Tioga Tension Disc 2008→2009, Girvin Flexstem 2007→2008, SRAM Grip Shift 2007→2008, battery light system 2012→2014, Kryptonite lock 1996→1997, Cannondale pannier 1998→1999, Cannondale Oval Seatstays 2002→2003, Biopace 2002→2003, Schwinn freestyle step 2005→2006, Browning sprocket shift 1995→1996, and three in the other direction where the old value had over-run the real term — Aheadset 2012→2010, chain scrubber 2006→2004, URT/Sweet Spot 2015→2014. Every affected entry was already `st:"expired"`, so no reader-facing filter ("Going free soon", the Stats watchlist) was misfiring — these were wrong numbers on cards, not a broken feature. Deliberately left alone: the Schwinn Sting-Ray banana seat (`US D204,121`, granted 1966, `exp:1970`), which is a 3½-year design-term election rather than a miscalculation, and the Mongoose Motomag (`US D236,609`), already correct at the flat 14-year design term.

- **`tools/verify_data.js` gained a patent-term check** so this class of drift is caught by tooling rather than by audit. It is informational and non-blocking, matching the bilingual check's precedent, because a mismatch is often a documented exception. Its scope is deliberately narrow — single-number, non-design, pre-1995 filings only — since post-1995 continuations legitimately run their clock from a parent application's filing date, and including them produced enough noise to bury the signal. Reports clean at the corrected baseline.

It is **US 4,889,521, "Multistage sprocket assembly for a bicycle," inventor Masashi Nagano, filed October 24, 1988, granted December 26, 1989**, assigned to Shimano Industrial Co. The grant year was also wrong in the data (`g:1990` → 1989), and `exp` moves 2010 → 2008 under the corrected term rule. The number was cross-confirmed across independent sources: the EPO family member EP 0313345 carrying the same title, and three later Shimano sprocket patents (US 6,923,741, US 8,177,670, US 9,376,165) whose own Background sections cite 4,889,521 by number as the Hyper Glide assembly.
